Proposition 4.48
.

\Ext_{\mathcal{E}(1)}^{s,t}({\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}R_{0}},\mathbb{Z}/2)
is given in
Figure

6
, right.

Proof.

Our proof uses as input
\Ext_{\mathcal{E}(1)}(N_{1})
, where
N_{1}
is defined to be the
\mathcal{A}
-module
\Sigma^{-1}\widetilde{H}^{*}(\mathbb{RP}^{2};\mathbb{Z}/2)
, with two
\mathbb{Z}/2
summands connected by a
\mathrm{Sq}^{1}
; this in turn defines its
\mathcal{A}(1)
- and

\mathcal{E}(1)
-module structures. Davis-Mahowald
[
DM81
, §2]
calculate
\Ext_{\mathcal{E}(1)}(N_{1})
as a graded vector
space but we also need its
H^{*,*}(\mathcal{E}(1))
-module structure.

Let
\langle Q_{1}\rangle\subset\mathcal{E}(1)
denote the subalgebra generated by
Q_{1}
, which is a two-dimensional vector space over

\mathbb{Z}/2
. As
\mathcal{E}(1)
-modules,
N_{1}\cong\mathcal{E}(1)\otimes_{\langle Q_{1}\rangle}\mathbb{Z}/2
, so by the change-of-rings
theorem (
3.4
), there are isomorphisms of
H^{*,*}(\mathcal{E}(1))
-modules

(4.49)

\Ext_{\mathcal{E}(1)}(N_{1})\cong\Ext_{\langle Q_{1}\rangle}(\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong\mathbb{Z}/2[v_{1}],

with
v_{1}\in\Ext_{\mathcal{E}(1)}^{1,3}(N_{1},\mathbb{Z}/2)
. The rightmost isomorphism in (
4.49
) uses Koszul
duality
[
BC18
, Remark 4.5.4]
, which applies because
\langle Q_{1}\rangle
is an exterior algebra.

Now for
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}R_{0}}
, we use the extension of
\mathcal{E}(1)
-modules

(4.50)

\textstyle{\displaystyle 0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\Sigma^{2}R_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}R_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}N_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\displaystyle 0,}

drawn in
Figure

6
, left.

\Sigma^{2}R_{0}
R_{0}
N_{1}

Figure 6
.
Left: the extension (
4.50
). Right: the long exact sequence it induces of Ext groups. See
the proof of
4.48
for why the long exact sequence looks like this; the key feature is that there are
no elements in odd topological degree, so all boundary maps vanish. The dashed lines are
h_{0}
-extensions which are
not implied by the long exact sequence, but are shown in the proof of
4.48
.

At first, all we know is
\Ext(N_{1})
. Because this lives solely in even topological degrees, and
\Sigma^{2}R_{0}
is

2
-connected, the long exact sequence diagram is empty in topological degree
1
, so the boundary map

(4.51)

\delta\colon\Ext_{\mathcal{E}(1)}^{s,s+1}({\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}R_{0}},\mathbb{Z}/2)\to\Ext_{\mathcal{E}(1)}^{s,s}(N_{1},\mathbb{Z}/2)

vanishes, which tells us the line
t-s=0
in
\Ext({\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}R_{0}})
consists of a single
\mathbb{Z}/2
summand
in filtration
0
. Therefore the line
t-s=2
in the long exact sequence diagram consists of two
\mathbb{Z}/2

summands: one in filtration
1
coming from
N_{1}
, and one in filtration
0
coming from
\Sigma^{2}R_{0}
. Since
the
1
-line of the diagram is empty and
\Ext(N_{1})
is concentrated in even degrees, the
3
-line of the diagram
is empty, so there are no differentials to the
2
-line. Continuing in this way produces
Figure

6
,
right.

Finally, acting by
h_{0}\in H^{*,*}(\mathcal{E}(1))
defines an isomorphism

(4.52)

\Ext_{\mathcal{E}(1)}^{0,2}({\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}R_{0}},\mathbb{Z}/2)\to\Ext_{\mathcal{E}(1)}^{1,3}({\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}R_{0}},\mathbb{Z}/2).

This can be checked directly from the definition: begin with the unique nontrivial map

{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}R_{0}}\to\Sigma^{2}\mathbb{Z}/2
and act on it by an extension representing
h_{0}
(namely the extension

0\to\Sigma\mathbb{Z}/2\to N_{1}\to\mathbb{Z}/2\to 0
); the result is a nontrivial extension.
∎

With
\Ext({\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\Sigma R_{0}})
in hand, we return to our goal of computing
\widetilde{\mathit{ku}}_{*}(X_{n})
.
We draw the
E_{2}
-page of the Adams spectral sequence in (
5
), right. Margolis’ theorem
(
3.22
) forces all differentials in this range to vanish, except possible differentials with target the

7
-line, and there can be no hidden extensions in the range depicted. Thus for
n=2k<7
,

\widetilde{\mathit{ku}}_{*}(X_{n})\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus k+1}
and for
n=2k+1<8
,
\widetilde{\mathit{ku}}_{*}(X_{n})\cong\mathbb{Z}/2^{k+1}
; we
finish with the fact that the map
\mathit{MTSpin}^{c}\to\mathit{ku}\vee\Sigma^{4}\mathit{ku}
is
7
-connected, so we can read off the spin
c
bordism groups from the
\mathit{ku}
-homology groups.
∎

Theorem 4.53
.

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{0}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(X_{n})

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{1}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(X_{n})

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{2}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(X_{n})

\displaystyle\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 2}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{3}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(X_{n})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/2n

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{4}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(X_{n})

\displaystyle\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 4},

\widetilde{\Omega}_{5}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(X_{n})
is torsion.

4.53
.

in

, left.

Left: the

,

.

. The

We calculated
\Ext(\mathbb{Z}/2)
in (
3.7
), and Adams-Priddy
[
AP76
, §3]
show

(4.55)

\Ext_{\mathcal{E}(1)}^{s,t}(\raisebox{0.0pt}{\rotatebox{180.0}{$Q$}},\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong\Ext_{\mathcal{E}(1)}^{s+1,t+1}(\mathbb{Z}/2,\mathbb{Z}/2),

with the isomorphism intertwining the
H^{*,*}(\mathcal{E}(1))
-actions.
We can therefore draw the
E_{2}
-page of the Adams spectral sequence in
Figure

7
, right. We hide most

v_{1}
-actions to declutter the diagram.

The first differential that could be nonzero is from the
2
-line to the
1
-line; as differentials are

h_{0}
-equivariant, if a
d_{r}
differential is nonzero on one summand in the tower on the
2
-line, then it is
nonzero on the entire tower, so we refer to differentials between towers. The May-Milgram theorem
[
MM81
]

characterizes differentials between towers: there is a
d_{r}
differential between those two towers iff the
Bockstein
\beta\colon H^{1}(\text{--};\mathbb{Z}/2^{r})\to H^{2}(\text{--};\mathbb{Z}/2)
carries a preimage of
Uy
to
Uw
. The Thom
isomorphism is natural with respect to this Bockstein, so it suffices to know whether
\beta(y)=w
in

H^{2}(BD_{2n};\mathbb{Z}/2)
, and we saw this in the proof of
4.32
, where
r
is the largest number
such that
2^{r}\mid n
. This means that the
2
-torsion in
\widetilde{\Omega}_{1}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(X_{n})
is isomorphic to that of
\mathbb{Z}/n
,
so along with our odd-torsion computation we see that
\widetilde{\Omega}_{1}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(X_{n})\cong\mathbb{Z}/n
.

The other differential we need to resolve in range goes from the tower in the
4
-line to the tower in the

3
-line. Action by
v_{1}\in\mathit{ku}_{2}
carries the tower in the
2
-line to the tower in the
4
-line, and the tower in
the
1
-line to the tower in the
3
-line, and differentials are
v_{1}
-equivariant, so there is also a
d_{r}

differential between these towers. As seen in
Figure

7
, right, on the
E_{\infty}
-page there are
r+1

{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.184,0.104}\mathbb{Z}/2}
summands on the
3
-line, all connected, so together with our odd-torsion
computation we see that
\widetilde{\Omega}_{3}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(X_{n})\cong\mathbb{Z}/2n
.

There can be no other nonzero differentials in range, and Margolis’ theorem precludes any hidden extensions, so we
are done.
∎

4.4.4.
Class A, spin-
1/2
case

Lemma 4.56
.

V_{\lambda}
is pin
c
iff
n
is odd.

Proof.

For
n
odd, we saw that inclusion of a reflection defines a map
B\mathbb{Z}/2\to BD_{2n}
which is an isomorphism on mod

2
cohomology. Therefore we can compute Stiefel-Whitney classes of
V_{\lambda}
by pulling back to
B\mathbb{Z}/2
, and we
saw that the pullback bundle is stably equivalent to a line bundle, so
w_{2}=0
.

For
n
even, recall that
V_{\lambda}
is pin
c
iff
\beta(w_{2}(V_{\lambda}))=0
, where
\beta\colon H^{k}(\text{--};\mathbb{Z}/2)\to H^{k+1}(\text{--};\mathbb{Z})
is the integral Bockstein.
3.28
means it suffices to show
\mathrm{Sq}^{1}(w_{2}(V_{\lambda}))\neq 0
. In the notation of
4.17
, for
n\equiv 2\bmod 4
,
w_{2}(V_{\lambda})=xy+y^{2}
, and
\mathrm{Sq}^{1}(xy+y^{2})=x^{2}y+xy^{2}\neq 0
. For
n\equiv 0\bmod 4
,
w_{2}(V_{\lambda})=w
, and by
4.30
,
\mathrm{Sq}^{1}(w)\neq 0
.
∎

Therefore for
n
odd, we consider
X_{n}\coloneqq(BD_{2n})^{2-V_{\lambda}}
. We computed
\Omega_{k}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(X_{n})
for

k\leq 4
in
4.45
.

For
n
even,
2.24
directs us to the spin
c
bordism of
X_{n}\coloneqq(BD_{2n})^{\mathrm{Det}(V_{\lambda})-1}
.

Theorem 4.57
.

If
n\equiv 2\bmod 4
, the first few spin
c
bordism groups of
X_{n}
are

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{0}^{{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}}(X_{n})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/2

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{1}^{{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}}(X_{n})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/n

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{2}^{{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}}(X_{n})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/4\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{3}^{{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}}(X_{n})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/n\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{4}^{{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}}(X_{n})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/8\oplus\mathbb{Z}/4\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2.

Because
3.24
implies
\widetilde{\Omega}_{5}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(X_{n})

\mathbb{Z}/n\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2
for

\mathbb{Z}/8\oplus\mathbb{Z}/4\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2
for

bordism groups of
\mathbb{Z}/2
. This may be of independent interest.

Proof.

We can read the odd-primary torsion off of
4.24
. For

and

, there is an isomorphism of
\mathcal{E}(1)

(4.58)

\widetilde{H}^{*}((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1}\wedge B\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\Sigma\mathcal{E}(1)}\oplus{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\Sigma^{2}R_{0}}\oplus{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\Sigma^{3}\mathcal{E}(1)}\oplus{\color[rgb]{0.45,0.01,0.92}\Sigma^{3}\mathcal{E}(1)}\oplus P,

where

is
4
-connected. Since we began with

by (

). We will add in the pin

bordism groups coming from this summand, which can be read off

[

, after running the Adams spectral sequence for the other summand.

Theorem 4.60
.

If
n\equiv 0\bmod 4
, the first few spin
c
bordism groups of
X_{n}
are

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{0}^{{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}}(X_{n})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/2

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{1}^{{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}}(X_{n})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/n

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{2}^{{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}}(X_{n})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/4\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{3}^{{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}}(X_{n})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/(n/2)\oplus(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 2}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{4}^{{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}}(X_{n})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/8\oplus\mathbb{Z}/4\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2.

Because
3.24
implies
\widetilde{\Omega}_{5}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(X_{n})
is torsion, the phase homology groups for this
symmetry type are
\mathbb{Z}/(n/2)\oplus(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 2}
for
d=2
and
\mathbb{Z}/8\oplus\mathbb{Z}/4\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2
for
d=3
.

Proof.

We closely follow the proof of
4.41
. For odd-primary torsion, use

4.24
to see that the odd-primary torsion in the range we care about is isomorphic to the
odd torsion in
\mathbb{Z}/n
in degrees
1
and
3
, and is
0
in degrees
0
,
2
, and
4
.

On to the prime
2
. In
4.41
, we established a splitting
X_{n}\simeq(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1}\vee M_{n}
, allowing us to focus solely on
\widetilde{\Omega}_{*}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(M_{n})
:
\mathit{MTSpin}^{c}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1}\simeq\mathit{MTPin}^{c}
(
2.10c
), and we know pin
c
bordism groups thanks to
Bahri-Gilkey
[
BG87a
,
BG87b
]
. In (
4.44
), we determined the
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module structure on

\widetilde{H}^{*}(M_{n};\mathbb{Z}/2)
in low degrees, and the isomorphisms of
\mathcal{E}(1)
-modules
R_{1}\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\oplus\Sigma R_{0}
and

\mathcal{A}(1)\cong\mathcal{E}(1)\oplus\Sigma^{2}\mathcal{E}(1)
mean that as
\mathcal{E}(1)
-modules,

(4.61)

\widetilde{H}^{*}(M_{n};\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong{\color[rgb]{0.576,0,0}\Sigma\mathbb{Z}/2}\oplus{\color[rgb]{0.776,0.064,0.024}\Sigma^{2}R_{0}}\oplus{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\Sigma^{2}\raisebox{0.0pt}{\rotatebox{180.0}{$Q$}}}\oplus{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\Sigma^{3}\mathcal{E}(1)}\oplus{\color[rgb]{0.45,0.01,0.92}\Sigma^{3}\mathcal{E}(1)}\oplus P,

where
P
is
4
-connected. A priori,
\Ext(P)
could have nonzero differentials to elements of the
4
-line, but
we will see that this does not happen without needing to compute
\Ext(P)
.
In
Figure

9
, left, we draw (
4.61
). To determine the

E_{2}
-page of the Adams spectral sequence, see (
3.7
) for
\Ext(\mathbb{Z}/2)
,
4.48
for

\Ext({\color[rgb]{0.776,0.064,0.024}R_{0}})
, and (
4.55
) for
\Ext({\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\raisebox{0.0pt}{\rotatebox{180.0}{$Q$}}})
. We draw the

E_{2}
-page of the Adams spectral sequence for
\widetilde{\mathit{ku}}_{*}(M_{n})
, as in
Figure

9
,
right — though for legibility, most
v_{1}
-actions are hidden.
3.24
implies there must be
differentials in this range, though not necessarily the
d_{2}
s pictured.

1
2
3

Uy
Uy^{2}

Figure 9
.
Left: the
\mathcal{E}(1)
-module structure on
\widetilde{H}^{*}(M_{n};\mathbb{Z}/2)
in low degrees. The pictured submodule contains
all elements in degrees
4
and below. The dashed line indicates a
\mathbb{Z}/2^{r}
Bockstein, which we use to resolve a
differential. Right: Ext of this submodule, which is the
E_{2}
-page of the Adams spectral
sequence computing
\widetilde{\mathit{ku}}_{*}(M_{n})
for
t-s\leq 4
. Most
v_{1}
-actions are hidden for readability. See the
proof of
4.60
for more information.

For
\widetilde{\Omega}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}_{1}(M_{n})
to be torsion, there must be a differential
d_{r}
from the
2
-line to the
1
-line;
then,
\widetilde{\Omega}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}_{1}(M_{n})\cong\mathbb{Z}/2^{r}
, and since
\Omega_{1}^{\mathrm{Pin}^{c}}\cong 0
,
\widetilde{\Omega}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}_{1}(M_{n})\cong\mathbb{Z}/2^{r}
as well. Differentials between towers, such as this
d_{r}
, are characterized by the May-Milgram
theorem
[
MM81
]
, and just as in the proof of
4.53
, we conclude
r
is the largest natural
number such that
2^{r}\mid n
. Combining this with our odd-torsion calculation,
\widetilde{\Omega}_{1}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(M_{n})\cong\mathbb{Z}/n
.

Continuing in increasing topological degree, this
d_{r}
kills the entire orange tower in the
2
-line, and we infer

\widetilde{\Omega}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}_{2}(M_{n})\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
. The green and blue summands in the
3
-line survive and split off by Margolis’
theorem.
v_{1}
-equivariance of differentials implies that
d_{r}\colon E_{2}^{s,4+s}\to E_{2}^{s+2,s+3}
is nonzero,
and again maps the orange tower to the dark red tower, leaving a single
{\color[rgb]{0.576,0,0}\mathbb{Z}/2}

summand in
E_{3}^{1,4}
. There can be no further differentials to the
3
-line, so
\widetilde{\Omega}^{{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}}_{3}(M_{n})_{2}^{\wedge}\cong\mathbb{Z}/2^{r-1}\oplus(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 2}
. Our odd-primary calculation then tells us that
\widetilde{\Omega}_{3}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(M_{n})\cong\mathbb{Z}/(n/2)\oplus(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 2}
. Finally, the orange tower in the
4
-line is killed by the
d_{r}
we most
recently discussed, and the two light red
{\color[rgb]{0.776,0.064,0.024}\mathbb{Z}/2}
summands in the
4
-line cannot
emit or receive differentials. Thus as promised
\Ext(P)
does not have nonzero differentials to the
4
-line, so
we conclude by adding the pin
c
bordism summands back in.
∎

4.4.5.
Comparison with
[
ZWY+20
]

Interacting fermionic phases equivariant for a dihedral group
D_{2n}
acting by rotations and reflections have
also been studied by Zhang-Wang-Yang-Qi-Gu
[
ZWY+20
]
, who considered both spinless and spin-
1/2
phases in
dimension
2+1
for all
n
, and in Altland-Zirnbauer class D. They also study systems without a spatial symmetry,
using the extended supercohomology classification of Wang-Gu
[
WG18
,
WG20
]
to classify these phases and
discuss the FCEP for dihedral groups. We find complete agreement with their results except for phases with spinless
fermions when
n\equiv 0\bmod 4
, where we predict
\mathbb{Z}/2\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2
and they predict
\mathbb{Z}/2
. This appears to arise
from a calculation error: as we note below in
4.62
, the comparison map between supercohomology and the
Anderson dual of spin bordism is an isomorphism for this symmetry type.

Remark 4.62
.

The phases we classify are realized by the extended supercohomology classifications of Wang-Gu
[
WG18
,
WG20
]

and Kapustin-Thorngren
[
KT17
]
.
22
22

22

These classifications concern phases with an internal
D_{2n}

symmetry, but the fermionic crystalline equivalence principle allows us to pass back and forth.

Gaiotto-Johnson-Freyd
[
GJF19
, §§5.4–5.6]
determine that the extended supercohomology classification à
la
[
KT17
,
WG18
]
is the cohomology of
(BD_{2n})^{2-V_{\lambda}}
or
(BD_{2n})^{\mathrm{Det}(V_{\lambda})-1}
with
respect to a spectrum they call
\mathrm{fGP}_{\leq 2}^{\times}
, which is equivalent to the
(-3)
-connected cover of

I_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathit{MTSpin}
. Wang-Gu’s refinement in
[
WG20
]
corresponds instead to the spectrum
\mathrm{fGP}^{\times}
,
equivalent to the
(-7)
-connected cover of
I_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathit{MTSpin}
.
23
23

23

The reader may at this point wonder why our
classification is a generalized
homology
theory, while these extended supercohomology classifications are
generalized
cohomology
theories. This is a subtle point. The passage between homology and cohomology occurs
because in these dimensions, we may approximate
\mathit{MTSpin}
by
\mathit{KO}
due to Anderson-Brown-Peterson’s
[
ABP67
]

study of the connectivity of the Atiyah-Bott-Shapiro map
[
ABS64
]
, then use that
\mathit{KO}
is shifted Anderson
self-dual
[
And69
,
FMS07
,
HS14
,
Ric16
,
HLN20
]
to pass between
I_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathit{KO}
-homology and
\Sigma^{4}\mathit{KO}
-cohomology.
See Freed-Hopkins
[
FH19a
, §5.1]
for further discussion.

The connective covering maps induce comparison maps from the classifications of fermionic phases using extended
supercohomology to the classification of fermionic phases under our ansatz. For
\mathrm{fGP}^{\times}
, the map is
sufficiently connected as to be an isomorphism between the classifications of
(d+1)
-dimensional phases for all

d\leq 5
. For
\mathrm{fGP}_{\leq 2}^{\times}
, the map is not always an isomorphism even for
d=2
: the cokernel
when computing supercohomology of
X
is
\widetilde{H}^{0}(X;\mathbb{Z})
, and this is nonzero e.g. for
X=(BC_{n})^{2-V_{\lambda}}

from §
4.3
. But for dihedral groups,
\widetilde{H}^{0}((BD_{2n})^{\xi};\mathbb{Z})
vanishes whenever
\xi\to BD_{2n}
is
a rank-
0
unorientable virtual vector bundle, so in this case the comparison map is an isomorphism.

4.5.
D_{2n}
acting by rotations

The dihedral group
D_{2n}
can act on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
in an orientation-preserving manner, where
C_{n}\subset D_{2n}
acts
by rotations in a plane and a preimage of the generator of
D_{2n}/C_{n}\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
acts by a rotation perpendicular
to that plane. Said differently, this point group is defined by a representation
\lambda\colon D_{2n}\to\mathrm{SO}_{3}

which decomposes as
\rho\oplus\sigma
, where
\rho
is the standard two-dimensional representation by rotations
and reflections, and
\sigma\colon D_{2n}\to\mathrm{O}_{1}
is the sign representation, which is the determinant of
\rho
.
Confusingly, this point group is sometimes called “three-dimensional dihedral symmetry;” in this convention, the
three-dimensional action by
\rho\oplus\underline{\mathbb{R}}
is called
pyramidal symmetry
.

As far as we know, interacting fermionic phases for this
D_{2n}
symmetry have not been studied in the literature
before.

Class D, spinless

Class D, spin-
1/2

Class A, spinless

Class A, spin-
1/2

n

§
4.5.1

§
4.5.2

§
4.5.3

§
4.5.4

0\bmod 4

{\hyperref@@ii[dihedral_twspin_bordism_0mod4]{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{Z}/}}}2

(Z/2)

⊕
2

0

(Z/2)

⊕
2

2\bmod 4

0

(Z/2)

⊕
2

0

(Z/2)

⊕
3

1,3\bmod 4

0

0

0

0

Table 5
.
D_{2n}
-equivariant phase homology groups, where
D_{2n}
acts faithfully on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
by rotations. These
arise as homotopy groups of Anderson duals of
\mathit{MTSpin}\wedge X_{n}
and
\mathit{MTSpin}^{c}\wedge X_{n}
, where
X_{n}
is one of

(BD_{2n})^{3-V_{\lambda}}
or
(BD_{2n})_{+}
. See §
4.5
for details and proofs.

For any representation
\phi\colon D_{2n}\to\mathrm{O}_{d}
, let
V_{\phi}\to BD_{2n}
denote the associated vector bundle.

Lemma 4.63
.

(1)

If
n
is odd,
V_{\lambda}
is pin
c
but not pin
-
.

(2)

If
n
is even,
V_{\lambda}
is not pin
c
.

Proof.

For (
2
), we show that if
\beta
is the integral Bockstein,
\beta w_{2}(V_{\lambda})\neq 0
. By

3.28
, it suffices to show
\mathrm{Sq}^{1}(w_{2}(V_{\lambda}))\neq 0
. For
n\equiv 2\bmod 4
,

(4.64a)

w_{2}(V_{\lambda})=w_{2}(V_{\rho})+w_{1}(V_{\rho})w_{1}(V_{\sigma})+w_{2}(V_{\sigma})=x^{2}+xy+y^{2},

and
\mathrm{Sq}^{1}(x^{2}+xy+y^{2})=x^{2}y+xy^{2}
. For
n\equiv 0\bmod 4
,

(4.64b)

w_{2}(V_{\lambda})=w_{2}(V_{\rho})+w_{1}(V_{\rho})w_{1}(V_{\sigma})+w_{2}(V_{\sigma})=w+x^{2},

and
\mathrm{Sq}^{1}(w+x^{2})=wx
, so in neither case is
V_{\lambda}
pin
c
.

Now (
1
). Choose
i\colon\mathbb{Z}/2\hookrightarrow D_{2n}
given by a splitting of
D_{2n}\twoheadrightarrow D_{2n}/C_{n}\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
; restricting to
\mathbb{Z}/2
along
i
,
\lambda
decomposes as
2\sigma\oplus\mathbb{R}
. Therefore

i^{*}V_{\lambda}\to B\mathbb{Z}/2
is spin
c
but not spin:
w_{2}(2V_{\sigma})=w_{1}(V_{\sigma})^{2}=x^{2}
, and for any vector bundle

V
,
V\oplus V
admits a complex structure, hence a spin
c
structure. In particular,
\beta(w_{2}(i^{*}V_{\lambda}))\neq 0
. The maps
\mathbb{Z}/2\hookrightarrow D_{2n}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{Z}/2
compose to the identity, so the induced maps on cohomology also compose to
the identity. Therefore
\beta(w_{2}(V_{\lambda}))\neq 0
too.
∎

These propositions are the analogues of
4.23
and
4.24
, helping us calculate
odd-primary torsion in phase homology groups.

Lemma 4.65

(Handel
[
Han93
, Theorems 5.2, 5.3]
)
.

(4.66)

\widetilde{H}_{k}(BD_{2n};\mathbb{Z}[1/2])\cong\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}/n,&k\equiv 3\bmod 4\\
0,&\text{\rm otherwise.}\end{cases}

As usual, Handel computes
H^{*}(BD_{2n};\mathbb{Z})
, and it is up to us to change to homology with
\mathbb{Z}[1/2]
coefficients.

Proposition 4.67
.

Suppose
V
is a rank-zero oriented virtual vector bundle.

(1)

The odd-torsion subgroup of
\widetilde{\Omega}_{k}^{\mathrm{Spin}}((BD_{2n})^{V})
is isomorphic to the
odd-torsion subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}/n
when
k=3
and vanishes for all other
k\leq 6
.

(2)

The odd-torsion subgroup of
\widetilde{\Omega}_{k}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}((BD_{2n})^{V})
is isomorphic to the
odd-torsion subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}/n
when
k=3
and
k=5
and vanishes for all other
k\leq 6
.

Proof.

It suffices to work at odd primes. There are odd-primary equivalences
\mathit{MTSpin}\to\mathit{MTSO}
and
\mathit{MTSpin}^{c}\to\mathit{MTSO}\wedge(B\mathrm{U}_{1})_{+}
; moreover, since
V
is oriented, there is a Thom isomorphism
\mathit{MTSO}\wedge(BD_{2n})_{+}\overset{\simeq}{\to}\mathit{MTSO}\wedge(BD_{2n})^{V}
. Therefore it suffices to study
\widetilde{\Omega}_{*}^{\mathrm{SO}}(BD_{2n})
for (
1
) and

\widetilde{\Omega}_{*}^{\mathrm{SO}}(BD_{2n}\wedge B\mathrm{U}_{1})
for (
2
) after completing at an odd prime
p
. Using

4.67
for input, as well as the Künneth formula to determine
H_{*}(BD_{2n}\wedge B\mathrm{U}_{1})_{p}^{\wedge}
, one sees that the Atiyah-Hirzebruch spectral sequences computing these bordism groups collapse
for degree reasons in total degree
6
and below.
∎

4.5.1.
Class D, spinless case

Let
f_{0}^{D}
denote the equivariant local system of symmetry types for this case.
2.11
tells us that
to compute
\mathit{Ph}_{*}^{D_{2n}}(\mathbb{R}^{3},f_{0}^{D})
, we should study the spin bordism of
X_{n}\coloneqq(BD_{n})^{3-V_{\lambda}}
.

Proposition 4.68
.

Suppose
n
is odd. Then

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{0}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(X_{n})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{1}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(X_{n})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/4

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{2}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(X_{n})

\displaystyle\cong 0

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{3}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(X_{n})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/n

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{4}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(X_{n})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{5}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(X_{n})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/16

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{6}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(X_{n})

\displaystyle\cong 0,

and therefore
\mathit{Ph}_{0}^{D_{2n}}(\mathbb{R}^{3},f_{0}^{D})\cong 0
.

Proof.

4.67
shows that
\widetilde{\Omega}_{k}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(X_{n})
lacks odd-primary torsion for
k=4,5
, so it
suffices to work at
2
. The inclusion
\mathbb{Z}/2\hookrightarrow D_{2n}
induces an isomorphism
H^{*}(BD_{2n};\mathbb{Z}/2)\to H^{*}(B\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}/2)
, as we saw in the proof of
4.20
, hence by naturality of the Thom isomorphism
gives an isomorphism

(4.69)

\widetilde{H}^{*}(X_{n};\mathbb{Z}/2)\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\widetilde{H}^{*}((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{3-V_{\lambda}|_{B\mathbb{Z}/2}};\mathbb{Z}/2).

Restricted to
\mathbb{Z}/2
,
\lambda\cong 2\sigma\oplus\mathbb{R}
, so by the stable Whitehead theorem, (
4.69
) gives a
stable
2
-primary equivalence
X_{n}\simeq(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{2-2\sigma}
. Campbell
[
Cam17
, §7.8]
computes

\widetilde{\Omega}_{k}^{\mathrm{Spin}}((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{2-2\sigma})
, obtaining the free and
2
-torsion summands we claim in the theorem
statement.
24
24

24

Campbell computes only through dimension
5
, and Beaudry-Campbell
[
BC18
, Figure 26]
shows
how to extend Campbell’s computation to dimension
6
.

∎

Proposition 4.70

(Pedrotti
[
Ped17
, Theorem 8.0.8]
)
.

For
n\equiv 2\bmod 4
,
\widetilde{\Omega}_{4}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(X_{n})\cong\mathbb{Z}
, and by
3.24

\widetilde{\Omega}_{5}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(X_{n})
is
torsion. Therefore
\mathit{Ph}_{0}^{D_{2n}}(\mathbb{R}^{3},f_{0}^{D})
vanishes.

Remark 4.71
.

Pedrotti reports this computation in terms of
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
of
3-V_{\lambda}
, rather than
\lambda
itself, so we
should check that our characteristic classes agree with his: we want
w_{1}(3-V_{\lambda})=0
and
w_{2}(3-V_{\lambda})=x^{2}+xy+y^{2}
. Indeed
\mathrm{Im}(\lambda)\subset\mathrm{SO}_{3}
, so
V_{\lambda}
is orientable, and from (
4.64a
) that

w_{2}(V_{\lambda})=x^{2}+xy+y^{2}
. Since
w_{1}(V_{\lambda})=0
, these are also
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
of
3-V_{\lambda}
, as
desired.

Proposition 4.72

(Pedrotti
[
Ped17
, Theorem 9.0.14]
)
.

For
n\equiv 0\bmod 4
,
\widetilde{\Omega}_{4}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(X_{n})\cong\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2
, and by
3.24

\widetilde{\Omega}_{5}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(X_{n})
is torsion. Therefore
\mathit{Ph}_{0}^{D_{2n}}(\mathbb{R}^{3},f_{0}^{D})\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
.

Pedrotti takes as input
w_{1}(3-V_{\lambda})=0
and
w_{2}(3-V_{\lambda})=w+x^{2}
, which agrees with the classes of

V_{\lambda}
(e.g. (
4.64b
)). Beware that what we call
x
he calls
y
, and vice versa!

4.5.2.
Class D, spin-
1/2
case

Let
f_{1/2}^{D}
denote the equivariant local system of symmetry types for this case.

4.63
and
2.11
tell us that to compute
\mathit{Ph}_{*}^{D_{2n}}(\mathbb{R}^{3},f_{1/2}^{D})
, we should study the
spin bordism of
(BD_{n})^{\mathrm{Det}(V_{\lambda})-1}
. Since
V_{\lambda}
is orientable, this is isomorphic to

\Omega_{4}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(BD_{2n})
.

Proposition 4.73
.

Suppose
n
is odd. Then
\Omega_{4}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(BD_{2n})\cong\mathbb{Z}
and
\Omega_{5}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(BD_{2n})\cong 0
, so

\mathit{Ph}_{0}^{D_{2n}}(\mathbb{R}^{3},f_{1/2}^{D})\cong 0
.

Proof.

The proof is almost the same as that of
4.68
: by
4.67
, there is no
odd-primary torsion, and
B\mathbb{Z}/2\to BD_{2n}
induces an isomorphism on mod
2
cohomology, hence also on
2
-local
spin bordism, and Mahowald-Milgram
[
MM76
]
show
\Omega_{4}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(B\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong\mathbb{Z}
and

\Omega_{5}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(B\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong 0
.
25
25

25

This is also been computed by other methods by Mahowald
[
Mah82
, Lemma
7.3]
, Bruner-Greenlees
[
BG10
, Example 7.3.1]
, Siegemeyer
[
Sie13
, Theorem 2.1.5]
, and
García-Etxebarria and Montero
[
GEM19
, (C.18)]
.

∎

Proposition 4.74
.

For
n
even,
\Omega_{4}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(BD_{2n})\cong\mathbb{Z}\oplus(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 2}
.

Proof.

Pedrotti
[
Ped17
, Theorems 8.0.4 and 9.0.3]
shows
\Omega_{4}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(BD_{2n})\cong\mathbb{Z}\oplus H_{4}(BD_{2n};\mathbb{Z})
, and
the latter is computed by Handel
[
Han93
, Theorem 5.2]
.
∎

Bruner-Greenlees
[
BG10
, Corollary 8.5.9]
also compute this when
n
is a power of
2
.

By
3.24
,
\Omega_{5}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(BD_{2n})
is torsion, so
\mathit{Ph}_{0}^{D}(\mathbb{R}^{3},f_{1/2}^{D})\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2
.

4.5.3.
Class A, spinless case

Let
f_{0}^{A}
denote the equivariant local system of symmetry types in the spinless type A case. By

4.63
, we should compute
\widetilde{\Omega}_{*}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(X_{n})
, where
X_{n}\coloneqq(BD_{2n})^{3-V_{\lambda}}
.

When
n
is odd,
V_{\lambda}
is spin
c
, so there is a Thom isomorphism
\mathit{MTSpin}^{c}\wedge X_{n}\simeq\mathit{MTSpin}^{c}\wedge(BD_{2n})_{+}
.

Theorem 4.75
.

Suppose
n
is odd. Then

\displaystyle\Omega_{0}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BD_{2n})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle\Omega_{1}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BD_{2n})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/2

\displaystyle\Omega_{2}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BD_{2n})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle\Omega_{3}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BD_{2n})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/4n

\displaystyle\Omega_{4}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BD_{2n})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}

\displaystyle\Omega_{5}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BD_{2n})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/8n\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2

\displaystyle\Omega_{6}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BD_{2n})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}.

Therefore
\mathit{Ph}_{0}^{D_{2n}}(\mathbb{R}^{3},f_{0}^{A})\cong 0
.

Proof.

4.67
accounts for the odd-primary torsion, so we just have to work at
2
. The map

\mathbb{Z}/2\hookrightarrow D_{2n}
induced by a choice of reflection defines an isomorphism on mod
2
cohomology, therefore by the
stable Whitehead theorem is a
2
-local stable equivalence. Therefore it defines an isomorphism

\Omega_{*}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(B\mathbb{Z}/2)_{2}^{\wedge}\to\Omega_{*}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BD_{2n})_{2}^{\wedge}
, and the spin
c
bordism of
B\mathbb{Z}/2
is
computed by Bahri-Gilkey
[
BG87a
,
BG87b
]
.
∎

Theorem 4.76
.

Suppose
n\equiv 2\bmod 4
. Then the first few spin
c
bordism groups of
X_{n}
are

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{0}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(X)

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{1}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(X)

\displaystyle\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 2}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{2}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(X)

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{3}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(X)

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/n\oplus(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 2}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{4}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(X)

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2},

and
\widetilde{\Omega}_{5}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(X)
is torsion.

Proof.

The odd-torsion subgroups can be read off of (
4.67
). For the
2
-primary part, we use the
Adams spectral sequence over
\mathcal{E}(1)
. Letting
U
denote the Thom class, we saw
w_{1}(V_{\lambda})=0
, so

\mathrm{Sq}^{1}(U)=0
, and (
4.64a
)
w_{2}(V_{\lambda})=x^{2}+xy+y^{2}
, so
\mathrm{Sq}^{2}(U)=U(x^{2}+xy+y^{2})
.
Using this and the Cartan formula, we have an
\mathcal{E}(1)
-module isomorphism

(4.77)

\widetilde{H}^{*}(X_{n};\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\raisebox{0.0pt}{\rotatebox{180.0}{$Q$}}}\oplus{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\Sigma\mathcal{E}(1)}\oplus{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.603,0.1072}\Sigma\mathcal{E}(1)}\oplus{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\Sigma^{3}\mathcal{E}(1)}\oplus{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\Sigma^{3}\mathcal{E}(1)}\oplus{\color[rgb]{0.45,0.01,0.92}\Sigma^{3}\mathcal{E}(1)}\oplus P,

where
P
is
4
-connected. We draw this in
Figure

10
, left. A priori
\Ext(P)
could have
nonzero differentials to the
4
-line and therefore affect our computation, but we will see that this cannot happen
without needing to determine
\Ext(P)
.

Theorem 4.78
.

When
n\equiv 0\bmod 4
, the first few spin
c
bordism groups of
X_{n}
are

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{0}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(X_{n})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{1}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(X_{n})

\displaystyle\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 2}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{2}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(X_{n})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{3}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(X_{n})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/n\oplus(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 2}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{4}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(X_{n})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2},

and
\widetilde{\Omega}_{5}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(X_{n})
is torsion. Therefore
\mathit{Ph}_{0}^{D_{2n}}(\mathbb{R}^{3},f_{0}^{A})\cong 0
.

Proof.

The odd-torsion subgroups are calculated in
4.67
. For the
2
-torsion, we use the Adams
spectral sequence over
\mathcal{E}(1)
. Recall that
w_{1}(V_{\lambda})=0
and (from (
4.64b
))
w_{2}(V_{\lambda})=w+x^{2}
, so
w_{1}(3-V_{\lambda})=0
and
w_{2}(3-V_{\lambda})=w+x^{2}
. Thus in
\widetilde{H}^{*}(X_{n};\mathbb{Z}/2)
,
\mathrm{Sq}^{1}(U)=0

and
\mathrm{Sq}^{2}(U)=U(w+x^{2})
. Using this and the Cartan formula, we can compute the
\mathcal{E}(1)
-action on

\widetilde{H}^{*}(X_{n};\mathbb{Z}/2)
, and find that

(4.79)

\widetilde{H}^{*}(X_{n};\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\raisebox{0.0pt}{\rotatebox{180.0}{$Q$}}}\oplus{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\Sigma\mathcal{E}(1)}\oplus{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.603,0.1072}\Sigma\mathcal{E}(1)}\oplus{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\Sigma^{3}\mathcal{E}(1)}\oplus{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\Sigma^{3}\mathcal{E}(1)}\oplus{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\Sigma^{3}\mathbb{Z}/2}\oplus{\color[rgb]{0.45,0.01,0.92}\Sigma^{4}\raisebox{0.0pt}{\rotatebox{180.0}{$Q$}}}\oplus P,

where
P
is
4
-connected. We draw this in
Figure

11
, left. We will see in a moment that
\Ext(P)

has no nonzero differentials to elements in degree
4
and below, which means we can ignore it in our computations.
We calculated
\Ext_{\mathcal{E}(1)}({\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\raisebox{0.0pt}{\rotatebox{180.0}{$Q$}}})
in (
4.55
), so we can draw the
E_{2}
-page of the
Adams spectral sequence in
Figure

, right.

.

\mathcal{E}(1)

when

. The pictured summand

and below. The gray dashed line indicates a

is

; this is not part of the

-page of the corresponding Adams spectral

;

that there is a

from the purple tower in the

is not always the

pictured.

Margolis’ theorem (
3.22
) implies the only possible nonzero differentials from an element of topological
degree
4
or below are the differentials from a tower in the
4
-line to the blue tower in the
3
-line, and

3.24
implies
\widetilde{ku}_{4}(X_{n})
has free rank
1
, so for some
r
this differential
d_{r}

is nonzero. Moreover, its source must be the purple tower: the red tower is in the image of
v_{1}\colon E_{2}^{s,s+2}\to E_{2}^{s+1,s+5}
, so if
d_{r}(x)=y
for any element
x
of the red tower in degree
4
, then
y
is
also in the image of
v_{1}
, but the blue tower is not in this image. Therefore we know that
d_{r}
kills the entire
purple tower in degree
4
, and the red tower survives to the
E_{\infty}
-page: the red tower supports no nonzero
differentials to the
3
-line, and if there were a differential from the
5
-line to the red tower,
h_{0}
-linearity
guarantees it would kill all but finitely many summands of the red tower, contradicting
3.24
.

It remains only to determine the value of
r
. In
H^{*}(BD_{2n};\mathbb{Z}/2)
, the
\mathbb{Z}/2^{k}
Bockstein carries (a preimage
of)
wy
to
w^{2}
, where
k
is the largest number such that
2^{k}\mid n
. This can be checked by, e.g., pulling
back to
BC_{n}
, where this Bockstein is discussed by
[
Cam17
,
DL20a
]
. The Thom isomorphism theorem implies the

\mathbb{Z}/2^{k}
Bockstein sends (a preimage of)
Uwy
to
Uw^{2}
, and therefore by the May-Milgram
theorem
[
MM81
]
,
r=k
.
∎

4.5.4.
Class A, spin-
1/2
case

Let
f_{1/2}^{A}
denote the equivariant local system of symmetry types in the spin-
1/2
type A case. In this case
the ansatz tells us to study
\Omega_{*}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BD_{2n})
.

Proposition 4.80
.

For
n
odd,
\mathit{Ph}_{0}^{D_{2n}}(\mathbb{R}^{3},f_{1/2}^{A})=0
.

Proof.

This follows from our computation of
\Omega_{k}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BD_{2n})
in
4.75
.
∎

Theorem 4.81
.

Suppose
n\equiv 2\bmod 4
. Then

\displaystyle\Omega_{0}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BD_{2n})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle\Omega_{1}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BD_{2n})

\displaystyle\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 2}

\displaystyle\Omega_{2}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BD_{2n})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2

\displaystyle\Omega_{3}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BD_{2n})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/n\oplus(\mathbb{Z}/4)^{\oplus 2}\oplus(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 2}

\displaystyle\Omega_{4}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BD_{2n})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}\oplus(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 3}

\displaystyle\Omega_{5}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BD_{2n})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/n\oplus(\mathbb{Z}/8)^{\oplus 2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}/4\oplus(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 5}

\displaystyle\Omega_{6}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BD_{2n})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}\oplus(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 6}.

Proof.

We calculated the odd-primary torsion in these bordism groups in
4.67
; now the
2
-primary
part. The inclusion
\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2\to D_{2n}
given by a reflection and a rotation by
\pi
induces an isomorphism
on mod
2
cohomology, so by the stable Whitehead theorem,
BD_{2n}\to B(\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2)
is an equivalence after
stabilizing and
2
-completing. Therefore
\Omega_{*}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BD_{2n})_{2}^{\wedge}\overset{\cong}{\to}\Omega_{*}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(B(\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2))_{2}^{\wedge}
; since
\mathit{MTSpin}^{c}\to\mathit{ku}\vee\Sigma^{4}\mathit{ku}
is an
isomorphism in degrees
8
and below, it suffices to know
\mathit{ku}_{*}(B(\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2))
.

Ossa
[
Oss89
, Proposition 3]
computes
\mathit{ku}_{*}(B(\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2))
by establishing an equivalence

(4.82)

\mathit{ku}\wedge B\mathbb{Z}/2\wedge B\mathbb{Z}/2\simeq(\mathit{ku}\wedge\Sigma^{2}B\mathbb{Z}/2)\vee\Sigma^{2}H(\mathbb{Z}/2[u,v]),

where the third term refers to a generalized Eilenberg-Mac Lane spectrum on the graded abelian group
\mathbb{Z}/2[u,v]
.
26
26

26

Ossa’s splitting (
4.82
) or its analogue on homotopy groups has also been proven in several other
ways: see Johnson-Wilson
[
JW97
]
, Bruner
[
Bru99
, Corollary 3.3]
, Bruner-Greenlees
[
BG03
, Example
4.11.2]
, Powell
[
Pow14
]
, and Bruner-Mira-Stanley-Snaith
[
BMSS15
, Theorem 2.12]
.
Using the stable
splitting

(4.83)

\Sigma^{\infty}(B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2)_{+}\simeq\mathbb{S}\vee\Sigma^{\infty}B\mathbb{Z}/2\vee\Sigma^{\infty}B\mathbb{Z}/2\vee\Sigma^{\infty}(B\mathbb{Z}/2\wedge B\mathbb{Z}/2),

we see that
\mathit{ku}_{*}(B(\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2))
can be assembled from the following pieces.

(1)

\mathit{ku}_{*}(\mathrm{pt})
, which contributes
\mathbb{Z}
in even degrees and
0
in odd degrees.

(2)

Two copies of
\widetilde{\mathit{ku}}_{*}(B\mathbb{Z}/2)
. Hashimoto
[
Has83
, Theorem 3.1]
shows each copy vanishes in
even degrees and is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}/2^{k+1}
in odd degree
2k+1
.

(3)

\widetilde{\mathit{ku}}_{*}(\Sigma^{2}B\mathbb{Z}/2)
. Hashimoto (
ibid
.) shows this vanishes in even degrees and
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}/2^{k}
in odd degree
2k+1
.

(4)

\pi_{*}(\Sigma^{2}H\mathbb{Z}/2[u,v])
, which contributes
0
in degrees
0
and
1
and
(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus(k-1)}
in
degrees
k\geq 2
.

Putting this together and adding in the odd-primary torsion, we obtain
\mathit{ku}_{k}(BD_{2n})
for
k\leq 6
; using the
Anderson-Brown-Peterson isomorphism
\Omega_{k}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BD_{2n})\cong\mathit{ku}_{k}(BD_{2n})\oplus\mathit{ku}_{k-4}(BD_{2n})
, valid
for
k<8
, we obtain the bordism groups in the theorem statement.
∎

Theorem 4.84
.

Suppose
n\equiv 0\bmod 4
. Then

\displaystyle\Omega_{0}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BD_{2n})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle\Omega_{1}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BD_{2n})

\displaystyle\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 2}

\displaystyle\Omega_{2}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BD_{2n})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2

\displaystyle\Omega_{3}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BD_{2n})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/n\oplus(\mathbb{Z}/4)^{\oplus 2}

\displaystyle\Omega_{4}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BD_{2n})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}\oplus(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 2},

and
\Omega_{5}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BD_{2n})
is torsion. Therefore
\mathit{Ph}_{0}^{D_{2n}}(\mathbb{R}^{3},f_{1/2}^{A})\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 2}
.

Proof.

Since
\Omega_{*}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BD_{2n})\cong\Omega_{*}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(\mathrm{pt})\oplus\widetilde{\Omega}_{*}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BD_{2n})
, we will focus on
\widetilde{\Omega}_{*}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BD_{2n})
, adding on

\Omega_{*}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(\mathrm{pt})
at the end. We also focus on the
2
-primary story: the odd-primary torsion is calculated
in
4.67
.

Recall from
4.17
that
H^{*}(BD_{2n};\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong\mathbb{Z}/2[x,y,w]/(xy+y^{2})
, with
\lvert x\rvert=\lvert y\rvert=1
and

\lvert w\rvert=2
. A choice of reflection induces a section of
BD_{2n}\to B\mathbb{Z}/2
, and therefore there is a spectrum

M_{n}
and a splitting

(4.85)

\Sigma^{\infty}BD_{2n}\overset{\simeq}{\longrightarrow}M_{n}\vee\Sigma^{\infty}B\mathbb{Z}/2,

such that as a subspace of
\widetilde{H}^{*}(BD_{2n};\mathbb{Z}/2)
,
\widetilde{H}^{*}(M_{n};\mathbb{Z}/2)
is complementary to the subspace
S
spanned by

\{x^{n}\mid n\geq 0\}
, because
S
is the image of the pullback map
\widetilde{H}^{*}(B\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}/2)\to\widetilde{H}^{*}(BD_{2n};\mathbb{Z}/2)
.
Bahri-Gilkey
[
BG87a
,
BG87b
]
show that
\widetilde{\Omega}_{4}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(B\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong 0
, so we just have to understand

\widetilde{\Omega}_{4}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(M_{n})
.

We will use the Adams spectral sequence over
\mathcal{E}(1)
to show that
\widetilde{\mathit{ku}}_{0}(M_{n})\cong 0
and

\widetilde{\mathit{ku}}_{4}(M_{n})\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 2}
, which suffices to prove the theorem. For degree reasons,
\mathrm{Sq}(x)=x+x^{2}
and
\mathrm{Sq}(y)=y+y^{2}
, and in
4.30
we saw
\mathrm{Sq}(w)=w+wx+w^{2}
. Using this, we find that
as
\mathcal{E}(1)
-modules,

(4.86)

\widetilde{H}^{*}(M_{n};\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\Sigma R_{0}}\oplus{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\Sigma^{2}\mathcal{E}(1)}\oplus{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.603,0.1072}\Sigma^{3}\raisebox{0.0pt}{\rotatebox{180.0}{$Q$}}}\oplus{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\Sigma^{4}\mathbb{Z}/2}\oplus{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\Sigma^{4}\mathcal{E}(1)}\oplus{\color[rgb]{0.45,0.01,0.92}\Sigma^{4}\mathcal{E}(1)}\oplus P,

where
P
is
5
-connected, and therefore too highly connected to affect our calculations. We draw this in

Figure

12
, left. We have already computed
\Ext(M)
for the remaining summands
M
: see

4.48
for
\Ext({\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}R_{0}})
, (
4.55
) for

\Ext({\color[rgb]{0.67,0.603,0.1072}\raisebox{0.0pt}{\rotatebox{180.0}{$Q$}}})
, and (
3.7
) for
\Ext({\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\mathbb{Z}/2})
. Therefore we
obtain the
E_{2}
-page of the Adams spectral sequence in
Figure

12
, right.

Table 6
.
Phase homology groups in dimension
3+1
equivariant with respect to various tetrahedral, octahedral, and
icosahedral symmetries and the ways they can mix with fermion parity. See the referenced sections for how the
fermionic crystalline equivalence principle associates this data with symmetry types for invertible TFTs.

5.1.
Chiral tetrahedral symmetry

We compute phase homology groups equivariant for a chiral tetrahedral symmetry
\lambda\colon A_{4}\to\mathrm{SO}_{3}
. As far
as we know, this point group has not yet been considered by physicists in the setting of fermionic phases. We will
show that our ansatz implies there are no nontrivial phases with either spinless or spin-
1/2
fermions in both
class D and class A. As usual,
V_{\lambda}\to BA_{4}
denotes the vector bundle associated to
\lambda
.

Proposition 5.1
.

H^{*}(BA_{4};\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong\mathbb{Z}/2[u,v,w]/(u^{3}+v^{2}+w^{2}+vw)
, where
\lvert u\rvert=2
and
\lvert v\rvert=\lvert w\rvert=3
.

\mathrm{Sq}(u)=u+v+w+u^{2}
,
\mathrm{Sq}(v)=v+u^{2}+uw+v^{2}
, and
\mathrm{Sq}(w)=w+u^{2}+uv+w^{2}
.

Except for the Steenrod operations, this result can be found in several places, such as
[
Kin
]

and
[
AM04
, Theorem III.1.3]
, so we will be brief.

Proof sketch.

Use the Lyndon-Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence
[
Lyn48
,
Ser50
,
HS53
]
for the short exact sequence
1\to\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2\to A_{4}\to\mathbb{Z}/3\to 1
; the mod 2 cohomology of
\mathbb{Z}/3
is trivial, so the spectral sequence collapses,
and

(5.2)

H^{*}(BA_{4};\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong H^{0}(B\mathbb{Z}/3;H^{*}(B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}/2))=H^{*}(B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\mathbb{Z}/3}.

We can choose this
\mathbb{Z}/3
-action to be such that a generator of
\mathbb{Z}/3
acts on
\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2=\{1,\alpha,\beta,\alpha+\beta\}
by
\alpha\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}\alpha+\beta
,
\beta\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}\alpha
, and

\alpha+\beta\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}\beta
. In a mild abuse of notation, we identify
\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2
with
H^{1}(B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong\Hom(\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2,\mathbb{Z}/2)
: these are dual
\mathbb{Z}/2
-vector spaces, and we have a basis for one,
which we identify with the dual basis vectors of the other. Thus
H^{*}(B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong\mathbb{Z}/2[\alpha,\beta]
.

The unique nonzero degree-
2
cohomology class fixed by
\mathbb{Z}/3
is
u\coloneqq\alpha^{2}+\alpha\beta+\beta^{2}
,
and two linearly independent degree-
3
classes fixed by
\mathbb{Z}/3
are
v\coloneqq\alpha^{3}+\alpha^{2}\beta+\beta^{3}

and
w\coloneqq\alpha^{3}+\alpha\beta^{2}+\beta^{3}
, whence the relation.

For the Steenrod squares, the identification in (
5.2
) of
H^{*}(BA_{4};\mathbb{Z}/2)
as a subalgebra of

H^{*}(B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}/2)
is the pullback map for
B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2\to BA_{4}
, hence
\mathcal{A}
-equivariant, so we
can compute
\mathrm{Sq}(u)
in
H^{*}(B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}/2)
; the computation follows from
\mathrm{Sq}(\alpha)=\alpha+\alpha^{2}

and
\mathrm{Sq}(\beta)=\beta+\beta^{2}
.
∎

Lemma 5.3
.

w_{1}(V_{\lambda})=0
and
w_{2}(V_{\lambda})=u
.

Proof.

Since
V_{\lambda}
is orientable,
w_{1}(V_{\lambda})=0
, and since
V_{\lambda}
is not spin,
w_{2}(V_{\lambda})\neq 0
.
Since
H^{2}(BA_{4};\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\cdot u
,
w_{2}(V_{\lambda})=u
.
∎

One way to see that this representation is not spin is to look at the
binary tetrahedral group

\mathit{2T}
, defined to be the preimage of
A_{4}\subset\mathrm{SO}_{3}
under the double cover
\mathrm{Spin}_{3}\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{SO}_{3}
. If

V_{\lambda}
were spin,
\mathit{2T}
would be a split extension of
A_{4}
by
\mu_{2}
, but it is not split.

5.1.1.
Class D, spinless case

If
A_{4}
does not mix with the symmetry type, our ansatz reduces to that of Freed-Hopkins, which reduces the
computation of these
A_{4}
-equivariant phase homology groups to the computation of
[\mathit{MTSpin}\wedge(BA_{4})^{3-V_{\lambda}},\Sigma^{5}I_{\mathbb{Z}}]
.

Theorem 5.4
.

The first few spin bordism groups of
X\coloneqq(BA_{4})^{3-V_{\lambda}}
are

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{0}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(X)

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{1}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(X)

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/3

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{2}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(X)

\displaystyle\cong 0

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{3}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(X)

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/6

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{4}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(X)

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{5}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(X)

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/18\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{6}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(X)

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/2

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{7}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(X)

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/9.

Thus if
f_{0}^{D}
denotes the
A_{4}
-equivariant local system of symmetry types for this case,
\mathit{Ph}_{0}^{A_{4}}(\mathbb{R}^{3},f_{0}^{D})=0
.

Proof.

At the prime
2
, we use the Adams spectral sequence; if
p
is an odd prime, the map

\widetilde{\Omega}_{*}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(X)\to\widetilde{\Omega}_{*}^{\mathrm{SO}}(X)
is an isomorphism on
p
-torsion, and we will determine the
p
-torsion
part of
\widetilde{\Omega}_{*}^{\mathrm{SO}}(X)
.

First, the
2
-primary piece.
Letting
U
denote the mod
2
Thom class as usual,
\mathrm{Sq}^{1}(U)=0
and
\mathrm{Sq}^{2}(U)=Uu
. This and

5.1
allow us to determine the
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module structure on
\widetilde{H}^{*}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)
in low degrees, as depicted in

Figure

, left.

Hence as
\mathcal{A}(1)

(5.5)

\widetilde{H}^{*}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\raisebox{0.0pt}{\rotatebox{180.0}{$Q$}}}\oplus{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\Sigma^{3}\mathcal{A}(1)}\oplus{\color[rgb]{0.45,0.01,0.92}\Sigma^{5}\mathcal{A}(1)}\oplus P,

Because we only care about degrees

) rules out nontrivial differentials and

Therefore the

For the odd-primary part, we use the fact that
\Omega_{*}^{\mathrm{Spin}}\to\Omega_{*}^{\mathrm{SO}}
is an equivalence after inverting

2
. Moreover, because
\lambda
factors through
\mathrm{SO}_{3}
,
V_{\lambda}\to BA_{4}
is orientable, so there is a Thom

\widetilde{\Omega}_{*}^{\mathrm{SO}}(X)\overset{\cong}{\to}\Omega_{*}^{\mathrm{SO}}(BA_{4})
. Hence we just need the odd-primary part of

\Omega_{*}^{\mathrm{SO}}(BA_{4})
, which is isomorphic to the odd-primary part of
\Omega_{*}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(BA_{4})
. In the degrees we care

\mathit{ko}_{*}(BA_{4})
, and Bruner-Greenlees

below degree

consists of
\mathbb{Z}/3

and
\mathbb{Z}/9

and

.
∎

5.1.2.
Class D, spin-
1/2
case

In this case, the symmetries mix as specified by the group extension giving the binary tetrahedral group.

Theorem 5.6
.

The
A_{4}
-equivariant phase homology group for the class D, spin-
1/2
symmetry type in 3d is trivial.

Proof.

Let
f_{1/2}^{D}
denote the local system on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
assigned to this symmetry type. Since
V_{\lambda}
is not pin
-
(if it were, it would be pin
-
and orientable, hence spin),
2.11
says
\mathit{Ph}_{0}^{A_{4}}(\mathbb{R}^{3};f_{1/2}^{D})\cong[\mathit{MTSpin}\wedge(BA_{4})_{+},\Sigma^{5}I_{\mathbb{Z}}]
. Bruner-Greenlees
[
BG10
, §7.7.E]
show

\mathit{ko}_{4}(BA_{4})\cong\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathit{ko}_{5}(BA_{4})
is torsion, so this phase homology group vanishes.
∎

5.1.3.
Class A

Let
f_{0}^{A}
and
f_{1/2}^{A}
be the
A_{4}
-equivariant local systems of symmetry types for spinless, resp. spin-
1/2

fermions in class A.

Lemma 5.7
.

V_{\lambda}\to BA_{4}
is not pin
c
.

Proof.

If
\beta\colon H^{2}(\text{--};\mathbb{Z}/2)\to H^{3}(\text{--};\mathbb{Z})
denotes the integral Bockstein, we want to show
\beta w_{2}(V_{\lambda})\neq 0
. By
3.28
, it suffices to show
\mathrm{Sq}^{1}(w_{2}(V_{\lambda}))\neq 0
.

5.3
gives
w_{2}(V_{\lambda})=b
, and
\mathrm{Sq}^{1}b=ab+c
.
∎

Therefore for spin-
1/2
fermions,
2.24
computes
\mathit{Ph}_{*}^{A_{4}}(\mathbb{R}^{3};f_{1/2}^{A})
in terms of the
spin
c
bordism of
(BA_{4})^{\mathrm{Det}(V_{\lambda})-1}
. Since
V_{\lambda}
is orientable, this is isomorphic to the spin
c
bordism of
BA_{4}
. For spinless fermions, we use
(BA_{4})^{3-V_{\lambda}}
, as usual.

Theorem 5.8
.

The low-degree spin
c
bordism groups of
X\coloneqq(BA_{4})^{3-V_{\lambda}}
and
BA_{4}
are

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{0}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(X)

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle\Omega_{0}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BA_{4})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{1}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(X)

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/3\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle\Omega_{1}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BA_{4})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/3

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{2}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(X)

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle\Omega_{2}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BA_{4})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{3}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(X)

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/6\oplus\mathbb{Z}/3\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle\Omega_{3}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BA_{4})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/6\oplus\mathbb{Z}/3

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{4}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(X)

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle\Omega_{4}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BA_{4})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2},

and in both cases,
\Omega_{5}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}
is torsion. Hence both
\mathit{Ph}_{0}^{A_{4}}(\mathbb{R}^{3};f_{0}^{A})
and
\mathit{Ph}_{0}^{A_{4}}(\mathbb{R}^{3};f_{1/2}^{A})
vanish.

Proof.

We use the equivalence
\mathit{MTSpin}^{c}\simeq\mathit{ku}\vee\Sigma^{4}\mathit{ku}
in degrees below
8
, then the Adams spectral sequence
over
\mathcal{E}(1)
to compute
\mathit{ku}
-homology at the prime
2
.

For the case of spinless fermions, use the
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module structure on
\widetilde{H}^{*}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)
from (
5.5
)
(drawn in
Figure

13
, left) to compute that the
\mathcal{E}(1)
-module structure is

(5.9)

\widetilde{H}^{*}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\raisebox{0.0pt}{\rotatebox{180.0}{$Q$}}}\oplus{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0}\Sigma^{3}\mathcal{E}(1)}\oplus{\color[rgb]{0.25,1,0.25}\Sigma^{5}\mathcal{E}(1)}\oplus{\color[rgb]{0.45,0.01,0.92}\Sigma^{5}\mathcal{E}(1)}\oplus P,

where
P
is
6
-connected. We draw this in
Figure

14
, left. We computed

\Ext_{\mathcal{E}(1)}^{s,t}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\raisebox{0.0pt}{\rotatebox{180.0}{$Q$}}},\mathbb{Z}/2)
in (
4.55
), and
P
is too high-degree to be
relevant to us, so the
E_{2}
-page of the Adams spectral sequence for
\widetilde{\mathit{ku}}_{*}(X)
is given in

Figure

14
, right. Margolis’ theorem (
3.22
) implies this spectral sequence collapses
and there are no extension problems, so we conclude.

.

\mathcal{E}(1)

in low degrees. The picture

, the

\widetilde{\mathit{ku}}_{*}((BA_{4})^{3-V_{\lambda}})
.

On to the spin-
1/2
case. As before,
\mathit{ku}_{*}(BA_{4})
splits as
\mathit{ku}_{*}(\mathrm{pt})\oplus\widetilde{\mathit{ku}}_{*}(BA_{4})
, and we focus
on the latter. Bruner-Greenlees
[
BG03
, §2.6]
show that
2
-locally, there is an equivalence

(5.10)

\mathit{ku}\wedge BA_{4}\simeq(\mathit{ku}\wedge\Sigma^{2}BC_{2})\vee\bigvee_{\alpha}\Sigma^{n_{\alpha}}H\mathbb{Z}/2

for some collection of integers
\alpha
; moreover, their calculation of
\mathit{ku}^{*}(BA_{4})

[
BG03
, Theorem 2.6.3]

implies the only
n_{\alpha}<8
(i.e. the ones relevant for us) are
n_{1}=2
and
n_{2}=6
. This, together with
Hashimoto’s computation of
\widetilde{\mathit{ku}}_{*}(B\mathbb{Z}/2)

[
Has83
, Theorem 3.1]
, tells us
\mathit{ku}_{*}(BA_{4})_{2}^{\wedge}
in
the degrees we need.

We still need to determine the odd-primary torsion.

Lemma 5.11
.

Let
p
be an odd prime; then, the inclusion
\mathbb{Z}/3\hookrightarrow A_{4}
sending a generator to
(1\ 2\ 3)
induces a

p
-primary stable equivalence
\Sigma^{\infty}(B\mathbb{Z}/3)_{+}\to\Sigma^{\infty}(BA_{4})_{+}
.

Proof.

Since
\lvert A_{4}\rvert=2^{2}\cdot 3
, for any
p\geq 5
, the maps
BA_{4}\to\mathrm{pt}
and
B\mathbb{Z}/3\to\mathrm{pt}
are
p
-local stable
equivalences, so we only have to address
p=3
. In this case,
3.27
implies the inclusion
j\colon\mathbb{Z}/3\hookrightarrow A_{4}
as the subgroup generated by
(1\ 2\ 3)
induces an isomorphism
H^{*}(BA_{4};\mathbb{Z}/3)\to H^{*}(B\mathbb{Z}/3;\mathbb{Z}/3)
,
so we conclude by the mod
p
Whitehead theorem
[
Ser53
, Chapitre III, Théorème 3]
.
∎

The Thom isomorphism theorem then implies
\widetilde{H}^{*}(X;\mathbb{Z}[1/2])\to\widetilde{H}^{*}((B\mathbb{Z}/3)^{3-j^{*}V_{\lambda}};\mathbb{Z}[1/2])
is an
isomorphism, so arguing in a similar way, there is a
p
-primary stable equivalence
X_{\lambda}\simeq(B\mathbb{Z}/3)^{3-V_{\lambda}}
. Thus, for the purpose of computing the odd-torsion subgroups of
\Omega_{*}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BA_{4})
and

\widetilde{\Omega}_{*}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(X)
, we can just work with
\mathbb{Z}/3
.

As a
\mathbb{Z}/3
-representation,
j^{*}V_{\lambda}
is isomorphic to the direct sum of a trivial representation and the real

2
-dimensional representation given by rotation. Each of these is spin
c
, the latter because it is unitary, so
there is a Thom isomorphism
\mathit{MTSpin}^{c}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/3)^{3-j^{*}V_{\lambda}}\cong\mathit{MTSpin}^{c}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/3)_{+}
, so in both the
spinless and spin-
1/2
cases, we just need the
3
-torsion in
\Omega_{*}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(B\mathbb{Z}/3)
, which we computed in

4.14
.
∎

5.2.
Pyritohedral symmetry

Pyritohedral symmetry
is the action of
G\coloneqq A_{4}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
in which
A_{4}
acts as the
orientation-preserving symmetries of a tetrahedron and
\mathbb{Z}/2
acts through inversion; let
\lambda
denote this
representation and
V_{\lambda}\to BG
be the associated vector bundle. Because
G
splits as a direct product, it is
easier to analyze than full tetrahedral symmetry (i.e. chiral tetrahedral symmetry together with a reflection), as
we will see in this and the next section.

5.2.1.
Spinless case

Let
X\coloneqq(BG)^{3-V_{\lambda}}
. By the twisted Künneth formula,
H^{*}(X)
is
2
-torsion; therefore

\widetilde{\Omega}_{*}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(X)
also lacks odd-primary torsion.
so we just have to work with the Adams spectral sequence at
p=2
. In the rest of this section, all cohomology is
with
\mathbb{Z}/2
coefficients unless otherwise stated.

Proposition 5.12
.

The first several spin bordism groups of
(BG)^{3-V_{\lambda}}
are

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{0}^{\mathrm{Spin}}((BG)^{3-V_{\lambda}})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/2

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{1}^{\mathrm{Spin}}((BG)^{3-V_{\lambda}})

\displaystyle\cong 0

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{2}^{\mathrm{Spin}}((BG)^{3-V_{\lambda}})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/2

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{3}^{\mathrm{Spin}}((BG)^{3-V_{\lambda}})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/2

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{4}^{\mathrm{Spin}}((BG)^{3-V_{\lambda}})

\displaystyle\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 3}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{5}^{\mathrm{Spin}}((BG)^{3-V_{\lambda}})

\displaystyle\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 3}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{6}^{\mathrm{Spin}}((BG)^{3-V_{\lambda}})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/16\oplus(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 2}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{7}^{\mathrm{Spin}}((BG)^{3-V_{\lambda}})

\displaystyle\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 2}.

Proof.

We employ a trick to reduce the amount of direct computations. We will replace
(3-V_{\lambda})\to BG
with a virtual vector
bundle
E\to BG
with the same first two Stiefel-Whitney classes, but which splits as an exterior sum over
BA_{4}

and
B\mathbb{Z}/2
. The Thom spectrum
(BG)^{E}
has two nice properties: the Adams
E_{2}
-page for calculating

\widetilde{\mathit{ko}}_{*}((BG)^{E})
is isomorphic to that of
\widetilde{\mathit{ko}}_{*}(X)
, but
(BG)^{E}
also splits as a smash
product of Thom spectra over
BA_{4}
and
B\mathbb{Z}/2
, simplifying the calculation of said
E_{2}
-page. Because we do not
construct a map from
\widetilde{\mathit{ko}}_{*}((BG)^{E})
to
\widetilde{\mathit{ko}}_{*}((BG)^{3-V_{\lambda}})
or vice versa, this
isomorphism does not allow us to deduce any differentials, but we will see that all differentials in range vanish
for formal reasons, so this is no problem.

The Künneth formula and
5.1
together imply

(5.13)

H^{*}(BG)\cong\mathbb{Z}/2[x,u,v,w]/(u^{3}+v^{2}+w^{2}+vw),

where
\lvert x\rvert=1
,
\lvert u\rvert=2
, and
\lvert v\rvert=\lvert w\rvert=3
, and that
\mathrm{Sq}(x)=x+x^{2}
and the Steenrod squares
of
u
,
v
, and
w
are as in
5.1
.

Lemma 5.14
.

The first two Steifel-Whitney classes of
V
are
w_{1}(V_{\lambda})=x
and
w_{2}(V_{\lambda})=u+x^{2}
.

Proof.

Since this representation contains orientation-reversing symmetries,
w_{1}(V_{\lambda})
must be nonzero, so is
x
.
For
w_{2}
, we saw in
5.3
that when one restricts to
A_{4}\subset A_{4}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
, one has

w_{2}(V_{\lambda}|_{BA_{4}})=u
; when one restricts to
\mathbb{Z}/2
, this is
3
copies of the sign representation, hence has

w_{2}(V_{\lambda}|_{B\mathbb{Z}/2})=x^{2}
.
∎

Let
E\to BG
be the virtual vector bundle

(5.15)

E\coloneqq 4-(V_{\lambda}|_{BA_{4}}\boxplus{-\sigma)},

where
\sigma\to B\mathbb{Z}/2
is the tautological line bundle. The Whitney sum formula implies for
i=1,2
,
w_{i}(E)=w_{i}(3-V_{\lambda})
. Feeding this to the Thom isomorphism gives isomorphisms of
\mathcal{A}(1)
-modules

(5.16)

\widetilde{H}^{*}((BG)^{3-V_{\lambda}})\cong\widetilde{H}^{*}((BG)^{E})

hence also isomorphisms of the
E_{2}
-pages of the corresponding Adams spectral sequences. Because

E\to BG
is an external sum,

(5.17)

(BG)^{E}\simeq(BA_{4})^{3-V_{\lambda}}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1}.

We know the
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module structures on the low-degree cohomology of both summands, and the Künneth formula tells
us to tensor them together (over
\mathbb{Z}/2
) to determine the
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module structure on
\widetilde{H}^{*}((BG)^{E})
.

In (
5.5
), we computed the
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module structure on
\widetilde{H}^{*}((BA_{4})^{3-V_{\lambda}})
in low
degrees, and split off two
\Sigma^{k}\mathcal{A}(1)
summands. Margolis’ theorem (
3.22
) promotes that to a
splitting of spectra

(5.18)

\mathit{ko}\wedge(BA_{4})^{3-V_{\lambda}}\simeq{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\Sigma^{3}H\mathbb{Z}/2}\vee{\color[rgb]{0.45,0.01,0.92}\Sigma^{5}H\mathbb{Z}/2}\vee{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}Y},

such that as an
\mathcal{A}
-module,

(5.19)

\widetilde{H}^{*}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}Y})\cong\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}(1)}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\raisebox{0.0pt}{\rotatebox{180.0}{$Q$}}}\oplus P),

where
P
is
7
-connected. When we smash
(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1}
back in, each
\Sigma^{k}H\mathbb{Z}/2\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1}
contributes a summand of
\widetilde{H}_{n-k}((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1})
to

\widetilde{\mathit{ko}}_{n}((BG)^{E})
, i.e. a
\mathbb{Z}/2
-summand in each degree
\ell\geq k
. The upshot for
\mathcal{A}(1)
-modules
is

(5.20)

\Sigma^{k}\mathcal{A}(1)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}/2}\widetilde{H}^{*}((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1})\cong\bigoplus_{\ell\geq k}\Sigma^{\ell}H\mathbb{Z}/2.

By (
5.16
), these summands are also present in
\widetilde{H}^{*}((BG)^{3-V_{\lambda}})
, and Margolis’ theorem
lifts this to split off corresponding
\Sigma^{\ell}H\mathbb{Z}/2
summands. Therefore there is a spectrum
Y^{\prime}
such that

(5.21)

\widetilde{\mathit{ko}}_{n}((BG)^{E})\cong\pi_{n}(Y^{\prime})\oplus{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\widetilde{H}_{n-3}((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1})}\oplus{\color[rgb]{0.45,0.01,0.92}\widetilde{H}_{n-5}((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1})}

and as
\mathcal{A}
-modules,

(5.22)

\widetilde{H}^{*}(Y^{\prime})\cong\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}(1)}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\raisebox{0.0pt}{\rotatebox{180.0}{$Q$}}}\oplus P)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}/2}\widetilde{H}^{*}((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1}).

The change-of-rings theorem (
3.4
) thus applies to the
E_{2}
-page of the Adams spectral sequence
calculating
\pi_{*}(Y^{\prime})
, yielding

(5.23)

E_{2}^{s,t}\cong\Ext_{\mathcal{A}(1)}^{s,t}(({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\raisebox{0.0pt}{\rotatebox{180.0}{$Q$}}}\oplus P)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}/2}\widetilde{H}^{*}((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1}),\mathbb{Z}/2).

We will work with this spectral sequence, adding in the summands corresponding to
{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\Sigma^{3}H\mathbb{Z}/2}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.45,0.01,0.92}\Sigma^{5}H\mathbb{Z}/2}
afterwards.

Our first order of business is to compute the tensor product in (
5.23
). The
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module
structure on
\widetilde{H}^{*}((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1})
can be found in
[
BC18
, Figure 4]
.

Lemma 5.24
.

There is an isomorphism of
\mathcal{A}(1)
-modules
\raisebox{0.0pt}{\rotatebox{180.0}{$Q$}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}/2}\widetilde{H}^{*}((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1})\cong\mathcal{A}(1)\oplus\Sigma^{2}R_{0}\oplus\Sigma^{4}\mathcal{A}(1)\oplus P
, where

is
7
-connected.

Proof.

Compute directly, by hand or by computer.
∎

By (

) and (

), we can work with (

), then add in the

Since

is
7
-connected, its Ext is concentrated in degrees irrelevant to us, and we ignore it.

\Ext({\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\Sigma^{2}R_{0}})
is computed in the degrees we need by Beaudry-Campbell

\begin{gathered}\includegraphics[]{crystalline_phases-figure21.pdf}\end{gathered}

Margolis’ theorem and

, so we conclude.

5.2.2.
Class D, spin-

Let

Theorem 5.27
.

The first few spin bordism groups of

are

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{0}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(X)

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/2

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{1}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(X)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{2}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(X)

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/8\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{3}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(X)

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{4}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(X)

Since

Let

.

5.3.
Full tetrahedral symmetry

The full group of symmetries of the tetrahedron, including reflections, is the symmetric group
S_{4}
, acting via
the representation
\lambda\colon S_{4}\to\mathrm{O}_{3}
, which is isomorphic to the quotient of the four-dimensional real
permutation representation by the fixed line
\mathbb{R}\cdot(1,1,1,1)
.

Proposition 5.34

(
[
Ngu09
, §2.3]
)
.

H^{*}(BS_{4};\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong\mathbb{Z}/2[a,b,c]/(ac)
, with
\lvert a\rvert=1
,
\lvert b\rvert=2
, and
\lvert c\rvert=3
. The Steenrod squares of
the generators are
\mathrm{Sq}(a)=a+a^{2}
,
\mathrm{Sq}(b)=b+ab+c+b^{2}
, and
\mathrm{Sq}(c)=c+bc+c^{2}
.
27
27

27

The ring
structure on
H^{*}(BS_{4};\mathbb{Z}/2)
was known earlier, due to Cardenas
[
Car65
]
; see
[
AM04
, Example VI.1.13]
.

Let
V_{\lambda}\to BS_{4}
denote the associated vector bundle to
\lambda
.

Proposition 5.35
.

w_{1}(V_{\lambda})=a
,
w_{2}(V_{\lambda})=b
, and
w_{3}(V_{\lambda})=c
.

Proof.

Since
\lambda
does not factor through
\mathrm{SO}_{3}\subset\mathrm{O}_{3}
,
V_{\lambda}
is unorientable. Thus
w_{1}(V_{\lambda})\neq 0
, and
a
is the only nonzero element of
H^{1}(BS_{4};\mathbb{Z}/2)
, so
w_{1}(V_{\lambda})=a
. For
w_{2}
, we calculated in

5.3
that
w_{2}(V_{\lambda}|_{A_{4}})\neq 0
, so
w_{2}
cannot vanish in
BS_{4}
. Our options are
a^{2}
,
b
, and

a^{2}+b
. Let
\mathbb{Z}/2\subset S_{4}
be generated by a transposition; then as a
\mathbb{Z}/2
-representation

\lambda\cong\mathbb{R}^{2}\oplus\sigma
, so
w_{2}(V_{\lambda}|_{\mathbb{Z}/2})=0
. The map
H^{*}(BS_{4};\mathbb{Z}/2)\to H^{*}(B\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong\mathbb{Z}/2[x]
sends
b,c\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}0
and
a\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}x
, so the constraint
w_{2}(V_{\lambda}|_{\mathbb{Z}/2})=0

rules out
w_{2}(V_{\lambda})=a^{2}
and
w_{2}(V_{\lambda})=a^{2}+b
, forcing us to conclude
w_{2}(V_{\lambda})=b
.
Finally,
w_{3}(V_{\lambda})=c
follows from the Wu formula.
∎

We need the next calculation to determine the odd-primary torsion subgroups of the phase homology groups that we
calculate.

Lemma 5.36
.

Suppose
V\to BS_{4}
is a rank-zero virtual vector bundle with
w_{1}(V)=x
. Then
the inclusion
i\colon S_{3}\hookrightarrow S_{4}
defines an isomorphism

(5.37)

\widetilde{H}_{*}((BS_{3})^{i^{*}V})\otimes\mathbb{Z}[1/2]\to\widetilde{H}_{*}((BS_{4})^{V})\otimes\mathbb{Z}[1/2].

Proof.

The commutative diagram of short exact sequences

induces a map between their Lyndon-Hochschild-Serre spectral sequences with signatures

(5.39)

E^{2}_{p,q}=H_{p}(B\mathbb{Z}/2;\underline{H_{q}(BA_{k};\mathbb{Z}[1/2])}\otimes(\mathbb{Z}[1/2])_{x})\Longrightarrow H_{p+q}(BS_{k};(\mathbb{Z}[1/2])_{w_{1}(V)}),

We claim the map on these spectral sequences is an isomorphism on
E^{2}
-pages. By
5.11
, the map

∎

Lemma 5.48

(Campbell

There is a noncanonical equivalence

. Thus, if

\tau_{0:4}\widetilde{\mathit{ko}}_{k}(Y)\cong\tau_{0:4}\widetilde{\mathit{ko}}{}^{k-3}(Y)

This is a corollary of the shifted self-equivalence

[

.

31

is torsion, so is isomorphic to

Theorem 5.49
.

\displaystyle H^{0}(BS_{4};(\mathbb{Z}_{(2)})_{w_{1}(V_{\lambda})})

\displaystyle\cong 0

\displaystyle H^{1}(BS_{4};(\mathbb{Z}_{(2)})_{w_{1}(V_{\lambda})})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/2

\displaystyle H^{2}(BS_{4};(\mathbb{Z}_{(2)})_{w_{1}(V_{\lambda})})

\displaystyle\cong 0

\displaystyle H^{3}(BS_{4};(\mathbb{Z}_{(2)})_{w_{1}(V_{\lambda})})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2

\displaystyle H^{4}(BS_{4};(\mathbb{Z}_{(2)})_{w_{1}(V_{\lambda})})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/2

\displaystyle H^{5}(BS_{4};(\mathbb{Z}_{(2)})_{w_{1}(V_{\lambda})})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2.

Proof.

Let
R\coloneqq\mathbb{Z}_{(2)}[x]/(x^{2}-1)
, which is a
\mathbb{Z}[C_{2}]
-module in
which the nontrivial element of
C_{2}
sends
1\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}1
and
x\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}-x
. As
\mathbb{Z}[C_{2}]
-modules,

R\cong\mathbb{Z}_{(2)}\oplus(\mathbb{Z}_{(2)})_{\sigma}
, so we will recover
H^{*}(BS_{4};(\mathbb{Z}_{(2)})_{w_{1}(V_{\lambda})})
from

H^{*}(BS_{4};R)
. The Lyndon-Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence

(5.50)

E_{2}^{*,*}=H^{*}(BC_{2};H^{*}(BA_{4};R))\Longrightarrow H^{*}(BS_{4};R)

is multiplicative; here
S_{4}
acts on
R
through
\mathit{sign}\colon S_{4}\to C_{2}
and
A_{4}
acts trivially.

R
is a
\mathbb{Z}/2
-graded ring, where
x
is in odd degree, and hence
R
-valued cohomology is
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
-graded.
We use
\{+,-\}
to denote the
\mathbb{Z}/2
-grading.

Proposition 5.51

(Čadek
[
Čad99
, Lemma 3.1]
)
.

There is an isomorphism of
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
-graded rings
H^{*}(BC_{2};R)\cong\mathbb{Z}_{(2)}[y]/(2y)
with
\lvert y\rvert=(1,-)
.

Proposition 5.52

(Bruner-Greenlees
[
BG03
, §2.6]
)
.

There is a presentation of
H^{*}(BA_{4};\mathbb{Z}_{(2)})
whose only generators and relations below degree
6
are generators

\alpha
and
\beta
in degrees
3
and
4
, respectively, and relations
2\alpha=2\beta=0
.

Corollary 5.53
.

As
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
-graded rings,

(5.54)

H^{*}(BA_{4};R)\cong\mathbb{Z}_{(2)}[\alpha_{+},\alpha_{-},\beta_{+},\beta_{-},\dots]/(2\alpha_{\pm},2\beta_{\pm},\dots)

where the generators and relations not displayed are in
\mathbb{Z}
-degrees
\geq 6
,
\lvert\alpha_{\pm}\rvert=(2,\pm)
, and

\lvert\beta_{\pm}\rvert=(3,\pm)
.

We can now display the

-page. Elements with

(5.55)

\begin{gathered}\includegraphics[]{crystalline_phases-figure29.pdf}\end{gathered}

The map

{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\alpha_{-}y^{2}}
.
∎

, left), and
r
and
\beta
follow from this and a few facts we just calculated for

\widetilde{H}^{*}(X;\mathbb{Z})
. For
k\leq 5
, we proved
2\widetilde{H}^{k}(X;\mathbb{Z})=0
, so
r
is injective in these degrees. Moreover,
combining this with
3.28
, that
r\circ\beta=\mathrm{Sq}^{1}
, we conclude for
k\leq 2
and

iff
\mathrm{Sq}^{1}\mathrm{Sq}^{2}(x)=0
.

All together, these allow us to resolve almost all of the indicated differentials — a priori, we do not know

\beta\mathrm{Sq}^{2}(x)
when
x\in E_{2}^{3,-2}\cong\widetilde{H}^{3}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)
, but for all
x
not in the image of

{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}d_{2}}\colon E_{2}^{1,-1}\to E_{2}^{3,-2}
,
\mathrm{Sq}^{2}(x)=0
, so this is fine. We find the

1
-line of the
E_{4}
-page has five
\mathbb{Z}/2
summands, one in
E_{4}^{2,-1}
, two in
E_{4}^{3,-2}
, and two in

E_{4}^{5,-4}
. There could be a nonzero
d_{4}\colon E_{4}^{2,-1}\to E_{4}^{6,-5}
, but the remaining four summands are
generated by permanent cycles.
∎

5.4.
Chiral octahedral symmetry

Let
\lambda\colon S_{4}\to\mathrm{O}_{3}
denote the representation as symmetries of an octahedron and
V_{\lambda}\to BS_{4}

denote the associated vector bundle. Recall from
5.34
the mod
2
cohomology of
BS^{4}
.

Lemma 5.66
.

w_{1}(V_{\lambda})=0
and
w_{2}(V_{\lambda})=b
.

Proof.

Since
\mathrm{Im}(\lambda)\subset\mathrm{SO}_{3}
,
w_{1}(V_{\lambda})=0
. We know
w_{2}(V_{\lambda})
restricts to
u\in H^{2}(BA_{4};\mathbb{Z}/2)

by considering tetrahedral symmetry inside octahedral symmetry and using
5.3
, so
w_{2}(V_{\lambda})
could
be
a^{2}+b
or
b
. The fact that
\lambda
splits as
\sigma\oplus\mathbb{R}^{2}
when restricted to a
\mathbb{Z}/2
subgroup given
by a transposition tells us
w_{2}(V_{\lambda})
is
b
, not
a^{2}+b
.
∎

By
5.7
, the pullback of
V_{\lambda}
to
BA_{4}
is not pin
c
, so
V_{\lambda}
is not pin
c
, and hence

V_{\lambda}
is also not pin
-
.

Lemma 5.67
.

(5.68)

\widetilde{\Omega}_{k}^{\mathrm{SO}}(BS_{4})\otimes\mathbb{Z}[1/2]\cong\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}/3,&k=3\\
0,&k=0,1,2,4,5,6.\end{cases}

Proof.

Let
\ell
be an odd prime and consider the Atiyah-Hirzebruch spectral sequence

(5.69)

E^{2}_{p,q}=H_{p}(BS_{4};(\mathit{MTSO}_{\ell}^{\wedge})_{q})\Longrightarrow(\mathit{MTSO}_{\ell}^{\wedge})_{p+q}(BS_{4})=\Omega_{p+q}^{\mathrm{SO}}(BS_{4})_{\ell}^{\wedge}.

If
\ell\neq 3
, then
\ell\nmid\lvert S_{4}\rvert
, so the
\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}
-cohomology of
BS_{4}
vanishes in positive degrees
and (
5.69
) is trivial, contributing no
\ell
-torsion to
\widetilde{\Omega}_{*}^{\mathrm{SO}}(BS_{4})\otimes\mathbb{Z}[1/2]
. For
\ell=3
, use Thomas’ calculation of
H^{*}(BS_{4};\mathbb{Z})

[
Tho74
]
and the universal coefficient theorem to show that

H_{*}(BS_{4};\mathbb{Z}_{3})
consists of
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
in degree
0
,
\mathbb{Z}/3
in degree
2
, and nothing else nonzero in degrees
5

and below. Therefore (
5.69
) collapses, giving us the desired result.
∎

5.4.2.
Class D, spin-
1/2
case

Let
f_{1/2}^{D}
be the
S_{4}
-equivariant local system of symmetry types for the case of spin-

in class D.
2.11
computes the equivariant phase homology of this local system in terms of

\Omega_{*}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(BS_{4})
.

Theorem 5.74
.

There is an
r\geq 2
such that the first several spin bordism groups of
BS_{4}
are

\displaystyle\Omega_{0}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(BS_{4})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle\Omega_{1}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(BS_{4})

\displaystyle\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 2}

\displaystyle\Omega_{2}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(BS_{4})

\displaystyle\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 3}

\displaystyle\Omega_{3}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(BS_{4})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/24\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2^{r+1},

\displaystyle\Omega_{4}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(BS_{4})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2

\displaystyle\Omega_{5}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(BS_{4})

\displaystyle\cong 0

\displaystyle\Omega_{6}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(BS_{4})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/2.

Therefore
\mathit{Ph}_{0}^{S_{4}}(\mathbb{R}^{3};f_{1/2}^{D})\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
.

One can use the Atiyah-Hirzebruch spectral sequence to show
r=2
in
5.74
; we do not need this
so do not present the proof.

Proof.

First, we use the Adams spectral sequence to determine the free and
2
-primary parts. Since
\mathit{ko}_{*}(BS_{4})
splits
as
\mathit{ko}_{*}(\mathrm{pt})\oplus\widetilde{\mathit{ko}}_{*}(BS_{4})
, we focus on
\widetilde{\mathit{ko}}_{*}(BS_{4})
and add the Bott-song summands in
at the end. There is a section
s
of the parity map
S_{4}\to\mathbb{Z}/2
, which stably splits
BS_{4}
. That is, there is a
spectrum
M
, a map
t\colon M\to\Sigma^{\infty}BS_{4}
, and a weak equivalence

(5.75)

(s,t)\colon\Sigma^{\infty}B\mathbb{Z}/2\vee M\overset{\simeq}{\longrightarrow}\Sigma^{\infty}BS_{4}.

This also splits the
\mathcal{A}
-module structure of
\widetilde{H}^{*}(BS_{4};\mathbb{Z}/2)
as
\widetilde{H}^{*}(M;\mathbb{Z}/2)\oplus\widetilde{H}^{*}(B\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}/2)
, where

\widetilde{H}^{*}(B\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}/2)
is embedded via the parity map. Therefore
\widetilde{H}^{*}(M;\mathbb{Z}/2)
is isomorphic to a complimentary
subspace of
\mathbb{Z}/2\cdot\{a^{k}\mid k\geq 0\}\subset\widetilde{H}^{*}(BS_{4};\mathbb{Z}/2)
. As this isomorphism is realized by a map of
spectra, it is an isomorphism of
\mathcal{A}
-modules, hence
\mathcal{A}(1)
-modules. We will run the Adams spectral sequence
for
\widetilde{\mathit{ko}}_{*}(M)
, and add the
\widetilde{\mathit{ko}}_{*}(B\mathbb{Z}/2)
summands in at the end.

The mod
2

BS_{4}
is given in

, and the action of the Steenrod squares in

5.35
. We can therefore draw
\widetilde{H}^{*}(M;\mathbb{Z}/2)
as an
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module in low degrees, which we do in

, left.

We have

(5.76)

\widetilde{H}^{*}(M;\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\Sigma^{2}J}\oplus{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\Sigma^{3}\raisebox{0.0pt}{\rotatebox{180.0}{$Q$}}}\oplus{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\Sigma^{4}\mathcal{A}(1)}\oplus{\color[rgb]{0.45,0.01,0.92}\Sigma^{6}\mathcal{A}(1)}\oplus P,

In the range pictured,

. Therefore there is

.

in degree
