.

To determine the odd-primary torsion, use first that the forgetful map
\Omega_{*}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(\text{--})\to\Omega_{*}^{\mathrm{SO}}(\text{--})
is
an isomorphism on odd-primary torsion, so we just have to determine the odd-primary torsion in

\Omega_{k}^{\mathrm{SO}}(BS_{4})
for
k\leq 6
, which we did in
5.67
.
∎

5.4.3.
Class A

As in the case of chiral tetrahedral symmetry,
V_{\lambda}
does not admit a pin
c
structure, since we saw in

5.7
that its pullback along
BA_{4}\to BS_{4}
also does not admit a pin
c
structure. Let
f_{0}^{A}
,
resp.
f_{1/2}^{A}
, denote the equivariant local systems of spectra associated to the class A spinless, resp. spin-
1/2
cases.
2.24
expresses
\mathit{Ph}_{0}^{S_{4}}(\mathbb{R}^{3};f_{0}^{A})
and
\mathit{Ph}_{0}^{S_{4}}(\mathbb{R}^{3};f_{1/2}^{A})
in
terms of the spin
c
bordism of
(BS_{4})^{3-V_{\lambda}}
for spinless fermions and
BS_{4}
for spin-
1/2
fermions.

Theorem 5.77
.

There are integers
r,r^{\prime}\geq 2
such that the low-degree spin
c
bordism groups of
(BS_{4})^{3-V_{\lambda}}
and
BS_{4}

are

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{0}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}((BS_{4})^{3-V_{\lambda}})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle\Omega_{0}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BS_{4})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{1}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}((BS_{4})^{3-V_{\lambda}})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle\Omega_{1}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BS_{4})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/2

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{2}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}((BS_{4})^{3-V_{\lambda}})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle\Omega_{2}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BS_{4})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{3}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}((BS_{4})^{3-V_{\lambda}})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/6\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2^{r}\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle\Omega_{3}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BS_{4})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/12\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2^{r^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{4}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}((BS_{4})^{3-V_{\lambda}})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle\Omega_{4}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BS_{4})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{5}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}((BS_{4})^{3-V_{\lambda}})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/2^{r-1}\oplus\mathbb{Z}/6\oplus(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 3}\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle\Omega_{5}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BS_{4})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\oplus\mathbb{Z}/24\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2^{r^{\prime}+1}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{6}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}((BS_{4})^{3-V_{\lambda}})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle\Omega_{6}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BS_{4})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}\oplus(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 3}.

One can use the Atiyah-Hirzebruch spectral sequence to show
r=r^{\prime}=2
. We do not need this, so do not go into
the details.

Proof.

As usual, the calculation separates into odd-primary and
2
-primary parts.

Lemma 5.78
.

The only odd-primary torsion in the spin
c
bordism of
(BS_{4})^{3-V_{\lambda}}
and
BS_{4}
in degrees
6
and below
consists of two
\mathbb{Z}/3
summands in degrees
3
and
5
.

Proof.

Since
\lvert S^{4}\rvert=2^{3}\cdot 3
, we only have to check
3
-torsion: if
\ell\geq 5
is prime, the maps
BS_{4}\to\mathrm{pt}

and
(BS_{4})^{3-V}\to\mathrm{pt}
are stable
\ell
-primary equivalences by the Whitehead theorem
[
Ser53
, Chapitre III,
Théorème 3]
. The forgetful map
\mathit{MTSpin}^{c}\to\mathit{MSO}\wedge(B\mathrm{U}_{1})_{+}
is an odd-primary equivalence, and since

3-V_{\lambda}
is orientable, there is a Thom isomorphism

(5.79)

\mathit{MSO}\wedge(B\mathrm{U}_{1})_{+}\wedge(BS_{4})^{3-V}\overset{\simeq}{\longrightarrow}\mathit{MSO}\wedge(B\mathrm{U}_{1})_{+}\wedge(BS_{4})_{+},

so in both the spinless and spin-
1/2
cases, we can glean the
3
-torsion from
\Omega_{*}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(B\mathrm{U}_{1}\times BS_{4})
. As the homology of
B\mathrm{U}_{1}
is torsion-free, the Künneth map
H_{*}(B\mathrm{U}_{1})\otimes H_{*}(BS_{4})\to H_{*}(B\mathrm{U}_{1}\times BS_{4})
is an isomorphism of graded abelian groups. Using this together with Thomas’
[
Tho74
]
calculation of

H_{*}(BS_{4})
, we conclude that the only odd-primary torsion in
H_{*}(B\mathrm{U}_{1}\times BS_{4})
in degrees below
7
is

\mathbb{Z}/3\subset H_{3}(B\mathrm{U}_{1}\times BS_{4})
and
\mathbb{Z}/3\subset H_{5}(B\mathrm{U}_{1}\times BS_{4})
.

Now feed this to the Atiyah-Hirzebruch spectral sequence with signature

(5.80)

E^{2}_{p,q}=H_{p}(B\mathrm{U}_{1}\times BS_{4},\Omega_{q}^{\mathrm{SO}}(\mathrm{pt}))\Longrightarrow\Omega_{p+q}^{\mathrm{SO}}(B\mathrm{U}_{1}\times BS_{4}).

The coefficients are sums of
\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathbb{Z}/2
; since we only care about
3
-torsion, we can ignore the
\mathbb{Z}/2

summands, whose differentials cannot map nontrivially to or from any
3
-torsion element. The only
3
-torsion on
the
E^{2}
-page in total degree less than
7
is a single
\mathbb{Z}/3
summand in each of
E^{2}_{3,0}
and
E^{2}_{5,0}
,
coming from our calculation above of
3
-torsion in homology. These
3
-torsion summands cannot participate in any
nonzero differentials: they do not map to each other, and cannot receive any differentials from free summands, or
from the
7
-line (which we have not calculated). Thus they persist to the
E^{\infty}
-page. It is a priori possible
more
3
-torsion is created from free summands on the
E^{2}
-page, which could happen if a differential maps from a
free summand to another free summand. All free summands are in even total degree, though, so this does not happen,
and the only
3
-torsion in
\Omega_{k}^{\mathrm{SO}}(B\mathrm{U}_{1}\times BS_{4})
, for
k<7
, is two
\mathbb{Z}/3
summands in degrees
3

and
5
.
∎

Next, we compute the
2
-torsion using the Adams spectral sequence over
\mathcal{E}(1)
.

For the spinless case, recall from (

) (drawn in

as an

-module.

-modules

(5.81)

where

5.5.
Full octahedral symmetry

The full group of symmetries of the octahedron, including orientation-reversing ones, is isomorphic to

G\coloneqq A_{4}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
. Let
\lambda\colon G\to\mathrm{O}_{3}
denote the corresponding three-dimensional real
representation of
G
, and
V_{\lambda}\to BG
denote the associated vector bundle. We saw in §
5.4

the pullback of
V_{\lambda}
along
BS_{4}\to BG
is not pin
c
, so
V_{\lambda}
is also not pin
c
, and therefore is
also not pin
-
.

The Künneth formula and
5.34
together imply

(5.83)

H^{*}(BG;\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong\mathbb{Z}/2[x,a,b,c]/(ac),

where
\lvert x\rvert=\lvert a\rvert=1
,
\lvert b\rvert=2
, and
\lvert c\rvert=3
.

Lemma 5.84
.

w_{1}(V_{\lambda})=x
and
w_{2}(V_{\lambda})=b+x^{2}
.

Proof.

For
w_{1}
, we know
w_{1}(V_{\lambda})\neq 0
because
V_{\lambda}
is unorientable, but because
V_{\lambda}|_{BS_{4}}
is
orientable,
w_{1}(V_{\lambda})
cannot be
a
or
x+a
, leaving
w_{1}(V_{\lambda})=x
.

For
w_{2}
, we know
the pullback of
V_{\lambda}
to
BS_{4}
has
w_{2}(V|_{S_{4}})=b
. If
i\colon B\mathbb{Z}/2\to BG
is induced by the inclusion
of a reflection in
G
, then
i^{*}\lambda
decomposes as a direct sum of three copies of the sign representation, so

i^{*}V_{\lambda}\cong 3\sigma
. Therefore
i^{*}w_{2}(V_{\lambda})=x^{2}
, uniquely constraining
w_{2}(V_{\lambda})=b+x^{2}
.
∎

5.5.1.
Class D, spinless case

The FCEP says we should study the spin bordism of
(BG)^{3-V_{\lambda}}
. We will argue as we did in the case of
pyritohedral symmetry in §
5.2
, replacing
3-V_{\lambda}
with a virtual vector bundle whose Adams
E_{2}
-page
is isomorphic to that of
(BG)^{3-V_{\lambda}}
, but which is easier to calculate. This isomorphism did not come from
a map of spectra, so cannot tell us anything about differentials or hidden extensions, but just as for pyritohedral
symmetry, we will see that for entirely formal reasons, all differentials vanish and all hidden extensions split in
the range we need. Using the twisted Künneth formula,
\widetilde{H}^{*}((BG)^{3-V_{\lambda}})
contains no odd-primary torsion,
so neither does
\widetilde{\Omega}_{*}^{\mathrm{Spin}}((BG)^{3-V_{\lambda}})
, so using the
2
-primary Adams spectral sequence suffices.

For the rest of this section, all homology and cohomology is understood to be with
\mathbb{Z}/2
coefficients.

Lemma 5.85
.

Let
E\to BG
denote the virtual vector bundle induced from the virtual representation

(5.86)

2-(V_{\lambda}|_{S_{4}}\boxplus(-\sigma)).

Then, there is an isomorphism of
\mathcal{A}(1)
-modules
\widetilde{H}^{*}((BG)^{3-V_{\lambda}})\cong\widetilde{H}^{*}((BG)^{E})
, hence
an isomorphism between the
E_{2}
-pages of the Adams spectral sequences for
\mathit{ko}\wedge(BG)^{3-V_{\lambda}}
and

\mathit{ko}\wedge(BG)^{E}
.

Proof.

The
E_{2}
-pages of these Adams spectral sequences are determined by the
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module structures on cohomology,
which are in turn determined by
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
of the virtual bundles
3-V_{\lambda}
and
E
. Since
w_{1}(E)=x
and
w_{2}(E)=u
, then for
i=1,2
,
w_{i}(3-V_{\lambda})=w_{i}(E)
.
∎

Because
E
is induced from a representation which is an exterior sum, its Thom spectrum splits as

(5.87)

(BG)^{E}\simeq(BS_{4})^{3-V_{\lambda}|_{S_{4}}}\wedge(B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1}

The Künneth theorem then simplifies the
E_{2}
-page:

(5.88)

E_{2}^{s,t}=\Ext_{\mathcal{A}(1)}^{s,t}(\widetilde{H}^{*}((BS_{4})^{3-V_{\lambda}|_{S_{4}}})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}/2}\widetilde{H}^{*}((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1}),\mathbb{Z}/2).

Campbell
[
Cam17
, Figure 6.1]
computes the
\mathcal{A}(1)
-module structure on
\widetilde{H}^{*}((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1})
, and we
computed
\widetilde{H}^{*}((BS_{4})^{3-V_{\lambda}})
in (
5.72
) (drawn in

Figure

21
).

Proposition 5.89
.

There is an isomorphism of
\mathcal{A}(1)
-modules

(5.90)

\widetilde{H}^{*}((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}/2}N_{4}\cong\Sigma N_{5}\oplus(V_{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}/2}\mathcal{A}(1))\oplus P_{2},

where
N_{5}
is as in
Figure

26
,
V_{2}
is a graded vector space with a homogeneous basis in degrees

\{0,2,3,4\}
, and
P_{2}
is
4
-connected.

Proof.

Compute directly, by hand or by computer.
∎

Recall from (
5.72
) (drawn in
Figure

21
) the

\mathcal{A}(1)
-module structure on
(BS_{4})^{3-V_{\lambda}}
. Margolis’ theorem (
3.22
) splits off a
\Sigma^{k}H\mathbb{Z}/2

summand from
\mathit{ko}\wedge(BS_{4})^{3-V_{\lambda}}
for every direct summand of
\Sigma^{k}\mathcal{A}(1)
in
\widetilde{H}^{*}((BS_{4})^{3-V_{\lambda}})
; below
degree
8
, this occurs for
k=3,5
. Therefore, by the same line of reasoning as in §
5.2
,
there is a spectrum
Y^{\prime}
such that

(5.91)

\widetilde{\mathit{ko}}_{n}((BG)^{3-V_{\lambda}})\cong\pi_{n}(Y^{\prime})\oplus{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\widetilde{H}_{n-3}((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1})}\oplus{\color[rgb]{0.45,0.01,0.92}\widetilde{H}_{n-5}((B\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\sigma-1})},

and as

To calculate the spin bordism groups of

.

The first few spin bordism groups of

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{0}^{\mathrm{Spin}}((BG)^{3-V_{\lambda}})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/2

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{1}^{\mathrm{Spin}}((BG)^{3-V_{\lambda}})

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{2}^{\mathrm{Spin}}((BG)^{3-V_{\lambda}})

5.5.3.
Class A, spinless case

Let
f_{0}^{A}
denote the local system of symmetry types for this case. We want to calculate

\widetilde{\Omega}_{*}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}((BG)^{3-V_{\lambda}})
. Using the twisted Künneth formula,

Theorem 5.101
.

The first few spin
c
bordism groups of
(BG)^{3-V_{\lambda}}
are:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{0}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}((BG)^{3-V_{\lambda}})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/2

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{1}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}((BG)^{3-V_{\lambda}})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/2

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{2}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}((BG)^{3-V_{\lambda}})

\displaystyle\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 3}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{3}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}((BG)^{3-V_{\lambda}})

\displaystyle\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 3}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{4}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}((BG)^{3-V_{\lambda}})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/4\oplus(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 4},

and
\widetilde{\Omega}_{5}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}((BG)^{3-V_{\lambda}})
is torsion. Hence
\mathit{Ph}_{0}^{S_{4}\times\mathbb{Z}/2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};f_{0}^{A})\cong\mathbb{Z}/4\oplus(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 4}
.

Proof.

There is an isomorphism of
\mathcal{E}(1)
-modules

(5.102)

N_{5}\cong\mathcal{E}(1)\oplus\Sigma R_{0}\oplus\Sigma^{2}R_{0},

hence another isomorphism of
\mathcal{E}(1)
-modules

(5.103)

\widetilde{H}^{*}((BG)^{3-V_{\lambda}})\cong(V_{c}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}/2}\mathcal{A}(1))\oplus{\color[rgb]{0.576,0,0}\Sigma^{2}R_{0}}\oplus{\color[rgb]{0.776,0.064,0.024}\Sigma^{3}R_{0}}\oplus P_{c},

where

is
4
-connected and

is a graded vector space with a homogeneous basis of elements in degrees

\{0,1,2,2,3,3,4,4,4,4\}
. Therefore the

-page of the Adams spectral sequence is

(5.104)

\begin{gathered}\includegraphics[]{round_2_sseq_6.pdf}\end{gathered}

Below degree

2
or higher.
And degree considerations rule out hidden extensions, so we are done.
∎

5.6.
Chiral icosahedral symmetry

Let
\lambda\colon A_{5}\to\mathrm{SO}_{3}
denote the representation given by chiral icosahedral symmetry, and as usual let

V_{\lambda}\to BA_{5}
denote the associated vector bundle.

Remark 5.108
.

Unlike the previous symmetry groups we studied, icosahedral symmetry is incompatible with translations, and there
are no space groups whose underlying point group is either the chiral icosahedral group or the full icosahedral
group. This means one should not expect to realize any phases equivariant for these symmetry groups as a lattice
Hamiltonian system on a periodic lattice on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. This does not rule out the possibility of interesting phases
with an icosahedral symmetry: there are examples of phases studied via lattice Hamiltonian realizations on lattices
in great generality, such as the toric code model in
[
Fre19
, §2.3]
, the GDS model in
[
FH16b
,
Deb20
,
FHHT20
]
, and the phases on aperiodic lattices studied by Huang-Wu-Liu
[
HWL20
]
. In a similar vein, it
may be possible for a Hamiltonian on an aperiodic lattice with icosahedral symmetry to model a nontrivial
crystalline SPT. See
[
VLP+19
]
for an example of how such an implementation might look.

For icosahedral symmetry, the hard work is behind us. Let
\lambda\colon A_{5}\to\mathrm{O}_{3}
denote the representation as
the orientation-preserving symmetries of the icosahedron. The restriction to
A_{4}\subset A_{5}
corresponds to
symmetries that preserve a concentric tetrahedron. Let
V_{\lambda}\to BA_{5}
be the associated bundle to
\lambda
.

Lemma 5.109
.

The inclusion
\varphi\colon A_{4}\hookrightarrow A_{5}
induces an equivalence on mod
2
cohomology. Hence

\varphi
induces
2
-primary equivalences
\Sigma^{\infty}(BA_{4})_{+}\to\Sigma^{\infty}(BA_{5})_{+}
and
(BA_{4})^{3-\varphi^{*}(V_{\lambda})}\to(BA_{5})^{3-V_{\lambda}}
.

Proof.

The first part is
3.27
: here
[A_{5}:A_{4}]=5
,
P=\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2
, and for both
A_{4}
and
A_{5}
,

N(P)/P\cong\mathbb{Z}/3
.

For the second part, the Thom isomorphism theorem tells us
\varphi^{\prime}\colon(BA_{4})^{3-\varphi^{*}(V_{\lambda})}\to(BA_{5})^{3-V_{\lambda}}
induces an isomorphism on mod
2
cohomology. The desired
2
-primary equivalences then
follow from the mod
2
Whitehead theorem
[
Ser53
, Chapitre III, Théorème 3]
.
∎

We can therefore reuse the calculations we made at the prime
2
in §
5.1
to obtain the
2
-primary
parts of
\widetilde{\Omega}_{k}^{\mathrm{Spin}}((BA_{5})^{3-V_{\lambda}})
and
\Omega_{k}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(BA_{5})
; the odd-primary pieces are different,
but not hard.

Proposition 5.110
.

The only odd-primary torsion in
H_{k}(BA_{5})
for
k<7
is contained in
H_{3}(BA_{5})\cong\mathbb{Z}/30
.

Proof sketch.

One can compute this using
Gap
; we also indicate how to do it by hand. Since
\lvert A_{5}\rvert=60=2^{2}\cdot 3\cdot 5
, there is no
p
-primary torsion for
p>5
, so it suffices to determine
H^{k}(BA_{5};\mathbb{Z}/3)
and

H^{k}(BA_{5};\mathbb{Z}/5)
in low degrees. This can be done using the theorem of Adem-Milgram
[
AM04
, Theorem II.6.8]

mentioned above, since the Sylow
3
- and
5
-subgroups of
A_{5}
are abelian.
∎

Corollary 5.111
.

In
\widetilde{\Omega}_{k}^{\mathrm{Spin}}((BA_{5})^{3-V_{\lambda}})
and
\Omega_{k}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(BA_{5})
, the only odd-primary torsion for
k<7
is a
\mathbb{Z}/15
in degree
3
.

Proof.

As usual, we use the fact that
\Omega_{*}^{\mathrm{Spin}}\to\Omega_{*}^{\mathrm{SO}}
is an isomorphism on odd-primary torsion, together
with the Thom isomorphism
\widetilde{\Omega}_{*}^{\mathrm{SO}}((BA_{5})^{3-V_{\lambda}})\cong\Omega_{*}^{\mathrm{SO}}(BA_{5})
, to reduce to showing the
claim for
\Omega_{k}^{\mathrm{SO}}(BA_{5})
. For this, use the Atiyah-Hirzebruch spectral sequence

(5.112)

E^{2}_{p,q}=H_{p}(BA_{5};\Omega_{q}^{\mathrm{SO}}(\mathrm{pt}))\Longrightarrow\Omega_{p+q}^{\mathrm{SO}}(BA_{5}).

On the
E^{2}
-page, the only odd-primary torsion in total degree below
7
is
\mathbb{Z}/15\subset E^{2}_{3,0}=H_{3}(BA_{5})
.
In all differentials involving
E^{r}_{3,0}
, the other group is zero, so this odd-primary torsion lives to the

E^{\infty}
-page.

We also must check that the free summands in total degree below
7
do not receive differentials that produce more
odd-primary torsion. There are only two such free summands, in
E^{2}_{0,0}
and
E^{2}_{0,4}
, and they can only
receive differentials from
2
-torsion abelian groups, so that does not happen.
∎

Now we need to combine this with the
2
-primary summands. For
(BA_{5})^{3-V_{\lambda}}
, we need

\Omega_{*}^{\mathrm{Spin}}((BA_{4})^{3-\varphi^{*}V_{\lambda}})
, which we computed in
5.4
. For
BA_{5}
, we need

\Omega_{*}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(BA_{4})
; in the degrees we need, this is isomorphic to
\mathit{ko}_{*}(BA_{4})
, which Bruner-Greenlees compute
in
[
BG10
, §7.7.E]
.

Theorem 5.113
.

The low-degree spin bordism groups of
(BA_{5})^{3-V}
and
BA_{5}
are

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{0}^{\mathrm{Spin}}((BA_{5})^{3-V_{\lambda}})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}\qquad

\displaystyle\Omega_{0}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(BA_{5})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{1}^{\mathrm{Spin}}((BA_{5})^{3-V_{\lambda}})

\displaystyle\cong 0\qquad

\displaystyle\Omega_{1}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(BA_{5})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/2

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{2}^{\mathrm{Spin}}((BA_{5})^{3-V_{\lambda}})

\displaystyle\cong 0\qquad

\displaystyle\Omega_{2}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(BA_{5})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{3}^{\mathrm{Spin}}((BA_{5})^{3-V_{\lambda}})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/30\qquad

\displaystyle\Omega_{3}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(BA_{5})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/60

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{4}^{\mathrm{Spin}}((BA_{5})^{3-V_{\lambda}})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}\qquad

\displaystyle\Omega_{4}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(BA_{5})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{5}^{\mathrm{Spin}}((BA_{5})^{3-V_{\lambda}})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2\qquad

\displaystyle\Omega_{5}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(BA_{5})

\displaystyle\cong 0

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{6}^{\mathrm{Spin}}((BA_{5})^{3-V_{\lambda}})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\qquad

\displaystyle\Omega_{6}^{\mathrm{Spin}}(BA_{5})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/2.

Hence the
0^{\mathrm{th}}

A_{5}
-equivariant phase homology groups vanish for both spinless and spin-
1/2

fermions.

Finally, class A. Since
V_{\lambda}
is not pin
c
, because its restriction to
A_{4}
is not (
5.7
),
we care about
(BA_{5})^{\mathrm{Det}(V_{\lambda})-1}\cong(BA_{5})_{+}
in the spin-
1/2
case, because
V_{\lambda}
is orientable.
Let
f_{0}^{A}
, resp.
f_{1/2}^{A}
, denote the equivariant local systems of symmetry types for the class A spinless,
resp. spin-
1/2
cases.

Theorem 5.114
.

The low-degree spin
c
bordism groups of
(BA_{5})^{3-V_{\lambda}}
and
BA_{5}
are

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{0}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}((BA_{5})^{3-V_{\lambda}})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}\qquad

\displaystyle\Omega_{0}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BA_{5})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{1}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}((BA_{5})^{3-V_{\lambda}})

\displaystyle\cong 0\qquad

\displaystyle\Omega_{1}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BA_{5})

\displaystyle\cong 0

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{2}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}((BA_{5})^{3-V_{\lambda}})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}\qquad

\displaystyle\Omega_{2}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BA_{5})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{3}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}((BA_{5})^{3-V_{\lambda}})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/30\qquad

\displaystyle\Omega_{3}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BA_{5})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/30

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{4}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}((BA_{5})^{3-V_{\lambda}})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}\qquad

\displaystyle\Omega_{4}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}(BA_{5})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2},

and in both cases,
\Omega_{5}^{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}
is torsion. Hence both
\mathit{Ph}_{0}^{A_{5}}(\mathbb{R}^{3};f^{A}_{0})
and
\mathit{Ph}_{0}^{A_{5}}(\mathbb{R}^{3};f^{A}_{1/2})
vanish.

Proof.

The calculation separates into
2
-primary and odd-primary computations; by
5.109
, the
2
-primary pieces
are exactly as in
5.8
.

The calculation of the odd-primary parts follows the same line of reasoning as the proof of

5.78
: as usual, use the odd-primary equivalence

\mathit{MTSpin}^{c}\to\mathit{MTSO}\wedge(B\mathrm{U}_{1})_{+}
. We know from
5.110
that the only odd-primary torsion in

H_{k}(BA_{5})
for
k\leq 6
is
\mathbb{Z}/15\subset H_{3}
; feeding that to the Künneth formula, the only odd-primary torsion
in
H_{k}(B\mathrm{U}_{1}\times BA_{5})
is two
\mathbb{Z}/15
summands in
H_{3}
and
H_{5}
. Then the Atiyah-Hirzebruch argument is
identical to the argument in
5.78
.
∎

5.7.
Full icosahedral symmetry

If one includes orientation-reversing symmetries of the icosahedron, the symmetry group enlarges to

A_{5}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
, with the
\mathbb{Z}/2
generated by an inversion. This symmetry group is also incompatible with
translations, so
5.108
applies. This calculation also quickly reduces to something we already know:
restricting the representation to
A_{4}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
yields the pyritohedral symmetry representation we studied in
§
5.2
.

Theorem 5.115
.

Let
\rho
be a virtual
A_{5}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
-representation with rank zero, and let
V_{\rho}\to BG
denote
the associated virtual vector bundle. Suppose that
w_{1}(V_{\rho})=x
, where
x
denotes the generator of

H^{1}(B\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}/2)\subset H^{1}(B(A_{5}\times\mathbb{Z}/2);\mathbb{Z}/2)
. Then inclusion of the pyritohedral symmetry subgroup

\varphi\colon A_{4}\times\mathbb{Z}/2\hookrightarrow A_{5}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
induces a homotopy equivalence

B(A_{4}\times\mathbb{Z}/2)^{V_{\rho}}\overset{\simeq}{\to}B(A_{5}\times\mathbb{Z}/2)^{V_{\rho}}
.

Proof.

By the Whitehead theorem, it suffices to establish that
\varphi
induces an isomorphism
\widetilde{H}^{*}(B(A_{5}\times\mathbb{Z}/2)^{V_{\rho}};k)\to\widetilde{H}^{*}(B(A_{4}\times\mathbb{Z}/2)^{V_{\rho}};k)
for
k=\mathbb{Q}
and
k=\mathbb{Z}/p
for all primes

p
.

5.109
and the Künneth theorem imply that
\varphi^{*}\colon H^{*}(B(A_{5}\times\mathbb{Z}/2);\mathbb{Z}/2)\to H^{*}(B(A_{4}\times\mathbb{Z}/2);\mathbb{Z}/2)
is an isomorphism. Together with the Thom isomorphism theorem, this takes care of the
case
k=\mathbb{Z}/2
.

Let
G
be either of
A_{4}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
or
A_{5}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
; the map
B\varphi\colon B(A_{4}\times\mathbb{Z}/2)\to B(A_{5}\times\mathbb{Z}/2)

allows us to think of
V_{\rho}
as over
BG
for either
G
, and make sense of the statement
w_{1}(V_{\rho})=x
. The
Thom isomorphism implies
\widetilde{H}^{*}((BG)^{V_{\rho}};\mathbb{Z})\cong H^{*}(BG;\mathbb{Z}_{x})
, and since
\mathbb{Z}_{x}
arises as a pullback local
system along
BG\to B\mathbb{Z}/2
, the twisted Künneth formula proves
\widetilde{H}^{*}(BG;\mathbb{Z})
is
2
-torsion. The universal
coefficient theorem then implies that when we take coefficients in
k=\mathbb{Q}
or
k=\mathbb{Z}/p
for
p
odd,

\widetilde{H}^{*}(B(A_{4}\times\mathbb{Z}/2)^{V_{\rho}};k)
and
H^{*}(B(A_{5}\times\mathbb{Z}/2)^{V_{\rho}};k)
vanish, so the map between them is
vacuously an isomorphism.
∎

Let
\lambda\colon A_{5}\times\mathbb{Z}/2\to\mathrm{O}_{3}
denote the representation as the group of symmetries of an icosahedron and

V_{\lambda}\to B(A_{5}\times\mathbb{Z}/2)
denote the associated vector bundle. Then
w_{1}(V_{\lambda})=x
. Let
f_{0}^{D}
and

f_{1/2}^{D}
denote the spinless, resp. spin-
1/2
class D equivariant local systems of symmetry types, and
f_{0}^{A}

and
f_{1/2}^{A}
denote their analogues in class A.

Corollary 5.116
.

\varphi
induces homotopy equivalences

(5.117a)

\displaystyle B(A_{4}\times\mathbb{Z}/2))^{3-V_{\lambda}}\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}(B(A_{5}\times\mathbb{Z}/2))^{3-V_{\lambda}}

(5.117b)

\displaystyle\ (B(A_{4}\times\mathbb{Z}/2))^{\mathrm{Det}(V_{\lambda})-1}\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}(B(A_{5}\times\mathbb{Z}/2))^{\mathrm{Det}(V_{\lambda})-1}.

Therefore

(1)

5.12
implies that
\mathit{Ph}_{0}^{A_{5}\times\mathbb{Z}/2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};f^{D}_{0})\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 3}
;

(2)

5.27
implies that
\mathit{Ph}_{0}^{A_{5}\times\mathbb{Z}/2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};f^{D}_{1/2})\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
;

(3)

5.29
implies that
\mathit{Ph}_{0}^{A_{5}\times\mathbb{Z}/2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};f^{A}_{0})\cong\mathbb{Z}/4\oplus(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 3}
; and

(4)

5.32
implies that
\mathit{Ph}_{0}^{A_{5}\times\mathbb{Z}/2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};f^{A}_{1/2})\cong\mathbb{Z}/8\oplus(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 3}
.

6.
Glide symmetry protected phases

Though we have focused on point group symmetries thus far, Freed-Hopkins’ ansatz
[
FH19a
]
also applies to
crystallographic groups. In this section, we apply their ansatz to the group of glide symmetries; invertible phases
equivariant for this symmetry have been studied by Lu-Shi-Lu
[
LSL17
]
and Xiong-Alexandradinata
[
XA18
]
,
and our results agree with theirs. In particular, Lu-Si-Lu make a conjecture classifying certain glide-symmetric
phases in all symmetry types, and we prove that their conjecture follows from Freed-Hopkins’ ansatz.

The group of
glide symmetries
acting on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
d\geq 2
, is the free group on the single generator

(6.1)

(x_{1},x_{2},\dotsc,x_{d})\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}(x_{1}+1,-x_{2},x_{3},\dotsc,x_{d}).

In previous sections, when the symmetry type is
H=\mathrm{Spin}
,
{\mathrm{Spin}^{c}}
,
\mathrm{Pin}^{\pm}
, etc., the symmetry type can mix
with the group action on spacetime, corresponding physically to spinless or spin-
1/2
fermions. Here, this cannot
happen: if
\mu_{2}
denotes the kernel of the map
\mathrm{Spin}_{n}\to\mathrm{SO}_{n}
or
\mathrm{Pin}_{n}^{\pm}\to\mathrm{O}_{n}
, all extensions

(6.2)

\textstyle{\displaystyle 0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\displaystyle\mu_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\displaystyle\widetilde{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\displaystyle 0}

split, so given one of these symmetry types, there is a unique equivariant symmetry type for this
\mathbb{Z}
-action with
respect to mixing with fermion parity, corresponding to the trivial local system
\underline{E}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
with value

E\coloneqq\mathrm{Map}(\mathit{MTH},\Sigma^{2}I_{\mathbb{Z}})
.

Definition 6.3
.

Recall from
1.28
that we defined a “forgetful map”
\varphi\colon\mathit{Ph}_{*}^{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\underline{E})\to\mathit{Ph}_{*}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\underline{E})
. The
intrinsically
\mathbb{Z}
-equivariant phase homology
, denoted

\widehat{\mathit{Ph}}{}_{*}^{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\underline{E})
, is the kernel of this map.

This corresponds under Freed-Hopkins’ ansatz to what Lu-Shi-Lu call a
glide SPT
: an invertible phase
equivariant for a
\mathbb{Z}
glide symmetry which is trivializable when one forgets the symmetry.

Let
\mathit{TP}_{d}(H)
denote the abelian group of SPT phases in (spatial) dimension
d
; Freed-Hopkins’ ansatz
[
FH16a
]

classifying these phases in terms of invertible field theories predicts
\mathit{TP}_{d}(H)\cong E_{-d}
.

Lu-Shi-Lu
[
LSL17
]
studied groups of glide SPTs and conjectured a formula classifying them in terms of the
classification of ordinary SPTs. We prove the corresponding statement on phase homology groups.

Theorem 6.4
.

For a given symmetry type
\rho_{n}\colon H_{n}\to\mathrm{O}_{n}
, there is a natural isomorphism
\widehat{\mathit{Ph}}{}_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\underline{E})\cong E_{-(d-1)}\otimes\mathbb{Z}/2
.

Passing this through the ansatz, this predicts that the group of glide SPTs is naturally isomorphic to

\mathit{TP}_{d-1}(H)\otimes\mathbb{Z}/2
, which is Lu-Shi-Lu’s original conjecture
[
LSL17
, Conjecture 1]
.
Xiong-Alexandradinata
[
XA18
]
also obtain this result using physics-based arguments.

Proof of
6.4
.

We calculate the
0^{\mathrm{th}}

\mathbb{Z}
-equivariant Borel-Moore
E
-homology of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. As the
\mathbb{Z}
-action
is free, this is the
0^{\mathrm{th}}
(nonequivariant) Borel-Moore
E
-homology of the fundamental domain

X\coloneqq\mathbb{R}^{d}/\mathbb{Z}
. Since the one-point compactification
\overline{X}
of
X
is a finite CW complex, this
Borel-Moore homology is isomorphic to
\widetilde{E}_{0}(\overline{X})
.

If
\sigma\to S^{1}
denotes the Möbius bundle, then
X
is diffeomorphic to the total space of

\sigma\oplus\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{d-2}\to S^{1}
, so
\overline{X}
is the Thom space

(S^{1})^{\sigma+d-2}
. The identification
(S^{1})^{\sigma}\cong\mathbb{RP}^{2}
induces
\overline{X}\cong\Sigma^{d-2}\mathbb{RP}^{2}
,
and therefore

(6.5)

\mathit{Ph}_{*}^{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\underline{E})\cong\widetilde{E}_{0}(\Sigma^{d-2}\mathbb{RP}^{2})\cong\widetilde{E}_{2-d}(\mathbb{RP}^{2}).

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
p\colon S^{2}\to\mathbb{RP}^{2}
be the double cover map and
s\colon\widetilde{E}_{k}(S^{1})\to\widetilde{E}_{k+1}(S^{2})
be the suspension
isomorphism. The composition
p_{*}\circ\delta\circ s\colon\widetilde{E}_{-1}(S^{2})\to\widetilde{E}_{-1}(S^{2})
is multiplication by
2
.

Proof.

This follows because the suspension is the cofiber of the cofiber; then one explicitly checks what happens on
mapping cylinders.
∎

Lemma 6.7
.

Under these isomorphisms, the forgetful map
\mathit{Ph}_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\underline{E})\to\mathit{Ph}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\underline{E})
is
identified with
\delta
.

Proof.

Because
\mathbb{Z}
acts freely on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
E_{0,\mathrm{BM}}^{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is identified with
\widetilde{E}_{0}
of the one-point
compactification of
\mathbb{R}^{d}/\mathbb{Z}
, which we saw above is homeomorphic to
\Sigma^{d-2}\mathbb{RP}^{2}
. The codomain of the
forgetful map is
E_{0,\mathrm{BM}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cong\widetilde{E}_{0}(\Sigma^{d-2}S^{2})
, so we have identified
\delta
with a map

\widetilde{E}_{0}(\Sigma^{d-2}\mathbb{RP}^{2})\to\widetilde{E}_{0}(\Sigma^{d-2}S^{2})
. But tracing through the construction in

1.28
, this map comes from applying
\widetilde{E}_{0}
to an actual map

\Sigma^{d-2}\mathbb{RP}^{2}\to\Sigma^{d-2}S^{2}
.

Next, precompose with
\Sigma^{d+2}p\colon\Sigma^{d-2}S^{2}\to\Sigma^{d-2}\mathbb{RP}^{2}
and check that this map has degree

2
, agreeing with
6.6
. This suffices to identify the maps because
p^{*}\colon[\mathbb{RP}^{2},S^{2}]\to[S^{2},S^{2}]

is injective.
∎

\mathbb{RP}^{2}
is homeomorphic to the cofiber of a degree-
2
map
S^{1}\to S^{1}
. Hence there is a long exact
sequence in reduced
E
-homology

(6.8)

\textstyle{\displaystyle\dotsb\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\displaystyle\widetilde{E}_{2-d}(S^{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m}
\textstyle{\displaystyle\widetilde{E}_{2-d}(S^{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r}
\textstyle{\displaystyle\widetilde{E}_{2-d}(\mathbb{RP}^{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\textstyle{\displaystyle\widetilde{E}_{1-d}(S^{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\displaystyle\dotsb}

where
m
is multiplication by
2
. Exactness implies
\ker(\delta)=\mathrm{Im}(r)=\coker(m)
. Using the suspension
isomorphism,
\widetilde{E}_{k}(S^{1})\cong\widetilde{E}_{k-1}
, and therefore
\coker(m)\cong E_{-(d-1)}\otimes\mathbb{Z}/2
, and

6.7
identifies
\delta
with the forgetful map from equivariant to nonequivariant phase homology for

\mathbb{R}^{d}
. In particular,
\widehat{\mathit{Ph}}{}_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\underline{E})\cong\ker(\delta)
, which we have naturally
identified with
E_{-(d-1)}\otimes\mathbb{Z}/2
.
∎

Remark 6.9
.

Using the long exact sequence (
6.8
), we observe that
\mathit{Ph}_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\underline{E})
has exponent

4
.
This is because for any long exact sequence of abelian groups

(6.10)

\textstyle{\displaystyle\dotsb\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\displaystyle A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cdot 2}
\textstyle{\displaystyle A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\displaystyle B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{\displaystyle C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cdot 2}
\textstyle{\displaystyle C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\displaystyle\dots}

in which
A
and
C
are finitely generated,
\mathrm{Im}(f)\cong A/2
, hence has exponent
2
, and
\ker(g)
is isomorphic
to the subgroup of order-
2
elements of
C
, which also has exponent
2
. Since
B
is an extension of
\ker(g)

by
\mathrm{Im}(f)
,
B
has exponent
4
.

Passing this observation through Freed-Hopkins’ ansatz, this recovers an observation of
Xiong-Alexandradinata
[
XA18
]
: that
any
phase equivariant with respect to glide symmetry, whether a
glide SPT or not, has order dividing
4
.

Example 6.11
.

In Altland-Zirnbauer class AII, corresponding to the symmetry type pin
\tilde{c}+
, the ansatz
predicts a unique nontrivial glide SPT in dimension
2+1
, coming from the classification

(6.12)

[\mathit{MTPin}^{\tilde{c}+},\Sigma^{4}I_{\mathbb{Z}}]\otimes\mathbb{Z}/2\cong\mathbb{Z}/2

(the calculation of
[\mathit{MTPin}^{\tilde{c}+},\Sigma^{4}I_{\mathbb{Z}}]
is due to Freed-Hopkins
[
FH16a
, §9.3]
). Physicists
are particularly interested in this nontrivial glide SPT phase, which is predicted to have unusual surface states
called “hourglass fermions”
[
WACB16
]
, and which has been studied experimentally
[
MYL+17
]
.

7.
Conclusion and outlook

We conclude by indicating a few directions of potential further research.

7.1.
From free fermions to interacting phases

Free fermion phases are a rich source of examples of invertible phases in the physics literature, at least for
symmetry types spin, pin
\pm
, spin
c
, etc. The classification of free fermion systems uses

K
-theory: see Kitaev
[
Kit09
]
for the original proposal and Freed-Moore
[
FM13
]
for a comprehensive
classification. However, for a given dimension and symmetry type, the map from free fermion systems to invertible
phases of matter can in general have both kernel (as first observed by Fidkowski-Kitaev
[
FK10
,
FK11
]
and
Turner-Pollmann-Berg
[
TPB11
]
) and cokernel (as first observed by Wang-Potter-Senthil
[
WPS14
]
and
Wang-Senthil
[
WS14
]
). Researchers are also interested in the free-to-interacting map for phases with spatial
symmetries, and this map has been studied from a physics point of view for crystalline phases in several works,
including
[
YR13
,
IF15
,
MFM15
,
LTH16
,
SS17
,
RL18
,
Zou18
,
LVK19
,
RS20
,
ZYQG20
,
ACR+21
]
.

Freed-Hopkins
[
FH16a
,
§9.2
,
§9.3
]
mathematically model the map
from free to interacting systems using the Atiyah-Bott-Shapiro map
\mathit{MTSpin}\to\mathit{KO}

[
ABS64
]
, but they do not
consider spatial symmetries. In view of the large bodies of research on free fermions with spatial symmetries and
invertible phases with spatial symmetries, it would be nice to understand the map between them in the presence of
spatial symmetry from the low-energy field theory perspective, and to make contact with the work of Adem, Antolín
Camarena, Semenoff, and Sheinbaum
[
AACSS16
]
, Sheinbaum and Antolín Camarena
[
SC20
]
, and
Cornfeld-Carmeli
[
CC21
]
studying free fermion phases with spatial symmetries using methods from homotopy
theory. This is something we hope to tackle in future work.

7.2.
Other symmetry types

We investigated two of the ten Altland-Zirnbauer classes, and it would be interesting to know whether a
version of the FCEP holds for other classes. One starting point could be class C, corresponding to a
spin
h
structure
[
FH16a
,
(9.25)
]
;
34
34

34

Spin
h

is the symmetry type
\mathrm{Spin}\times_{\mu_{2}}\mathrm{SU}_{2}\to\mathrm{O}
. Freed-Hopkins
[
FH16a
,
Proposition
9.16
]
call this symmetry type
G^{0}
; it is sometimes also called spin-
\mathrm{SU}_{2}
, e.g. in
[
WWW19
]
.
Likewise, the symmetry types pin
h\pm
we refer to later in this section are defined to be

\mathrm{Pin}^{\pm}\times_{\mu_{2}}\mathrm{SU}_{2}
, and are called
G^{\pm}
by Freed-Hopkins
[
FH16a
,
Proposition
9.16
]
.
the calculations in §
2.8
could be applied to
\mathrm{Spin}_{n}^{h}
to obtain a fermionic crystalline
equivalence principle for class C and hopefully phase homology calculations predicting the existence of additional
crystalline SPT phases.

Several teams of researchers have studied or classified interacting fermionic crystalline SPTs for other
Altland-Zirnbauer types, including
[
YR13
,
YX14
,
CHMR15
,
LTH16
,
WF17
,
CW18
,
RL18
,
SXG18
,
MSH19
,
ZXXS20
,
ZYQG20
]
. It would be good to compare their computations with the predictions made by an FCEP in other symmetry
types.

Another interesting potential connection with preexisting work is the case of class A phases with a spatial
reflection interacting with the internal
\mathrm{U}_{1}
symmetry. Depending on how the symmetries mix,
Shiozaki-Shapourian-Gomi-Ryu
[
SSGR18
, §V.C, §V.E]
and Thorngren-Else
[
TE18
, §VII.B]
obtain
classifications in terms of pin
\tilde{c}\pm
bordism, and we would be interested in knowing
whether that can also be obtained from our ansatz. Similarly, can one begin with class C phases and a reflection
acting on the internal
\mathrm{SU}_{2}
symmetry and obtain a classification in terms of pin
h\pm

bordism?

7.3.
Crystallographic groups

Though we discussed glide symmetries in §
6
, we have barely touched upon the rich world of
crystallographic groups. Free-fermion phases equivariant for these groups have been studied, e.g. in
[
SMJZ13
,
KdBvW+17
,
SSG18
,
OSS19
]
, but much less is known about the interacting case, even though the our
ansatz applies to it. There are some classifications by other methods for various classes of crystallographic
groups; for example, Ouyang-Wang-Gu-Qi
[
OWGQ20
]
study wallpaper group symmetries, and Sheinbaum-Antolín
Camarena
[
SC20
]
provide a general framework and a few examples. There is also work by
Wang-Alexandradinata-Cava-Bernevig
[
WACB16
]
and Guo-Ohmori-Putrov-Wan-Wang
[
GOP+20
]
studying interacting
phases for specific crystallographic groups that are not point groups.

7.4.
Lattice realizations

Modeling topological phases as lattice Hamiltonian systems can make any crystallographic symmetries acting on space
very explicit, using a lattice and Hamiltonian invariant under the symmetry of interest. Our predictions of
point group SPTs should correspond to actual lattice models of phases. We listed several specific predicted phases
of interest in §
3.1
, and these would make for good starting points for lattice
realizations.
